当然，还要谢谢他的好意。”
“是——呢，您不能替我说么，叔叔？”
“你自己说，样子更好。”
“是，也许。他会很恼火么？”
“可能，”彼得勋爵说，“不过就算恼火，他也不会露出来。准备好了？”
咨询委员会于是回到书房。波普先生已审慎地从壁炉地毯撤防，正在察看远处的一只书柜。
“多谢您的好意，波普先生，”子爵昂然大步走上前去，“可我考虑过了，我——我——我喜欢上了这本书，决定不卖。”
“抱歉之至，云云，”彼得勋爵插话，“可我外甥铁了心。不，不是价钱的事；他就是想要这书。但愿能效劳，可这事不在我手里。走之前不再来点什么？真不？按铃，小黄瓜。我的人会送您到电梯口。晚安。”
客人走后，彼得勋爵回来，若有所思地拿起书。
“把他单独留在书旁边，哪怕一分钟，咱们也是蠢透了，小黄瓜。所幸没出岔子。”
“您不觉得我们出去那会儿他查出了什么吧，叔叔？”小黄瓜瞪圆了眼。
“我确信没有。”
“为什么？”
“送他出门的半道上，他开价五十镑买它。自己把底泄了。嗯！邦特。”
“爵爷？”
“把这本书放进保险柜，钥匙拿来还我。今晚锁门时，最好把防贼警铃全上上。”
“哦——啊！”圣乔治子爵说。
---
威尔伯福斯·波普先生来访后的第三个早晨，子爵在舅舅的公寓里吃着迟迟的早餐——此前是一个男孩所能经历的最辉煌、最荡气回肠的夜晚。他激动得几乎吃不下邦特端来的腰子熏肉；而邦特那一贯无懈可击的仪态，丝毫没有因为一只迅速肿起发青的眼睛而减色。
凌晨两点钟左右，小黄瓜——前一晚的晚餐和戏剧太铺张、太成人化，他睡得不沉——察觉太平梯方向有一阵鬼祟的响动。他下了床，蹑手蹑脚溜进彼得勋爵的房间，叫醒了他。他说：“彼得叔叔，我敢肯定，太平梯上有贼。”

===Original===

And Uncle Peter, instead of saying, “Nonsense, Gherkins, hurry up and get back to bed,” had sat up and listened and said: “By Jove, Gherkins, I believe you’re right.” And had sent Gherkins to call Bunter. And on his return, Gherkins, who had always regarded his uncle as a very top-hatted sort of person, actually saw him take from his handkerchief-drawer an undeniable automatic pistol.
It was at this point that Lord Peter was apotheosed from the state of Quite Decent Uncle to that of Glorified Uncle. He said:
“Look here, Gherkins, we don’t know how many of these blighters there’ll be, so you must be jolly smart and do anything I say sharp, on the word of command﻿—even if I have to say ‘Scoot.’ Promise?”
Gherkins promised, with his heart thumping, and they sat waiting in the dark, till suddenly a little electric bell rang sharply just over the head of Lord Peter’s bed and a green light shone out.
“The library window,” said his lordship, promptly silencing the bell by turning a switch. “If they heard, they may think better of it. We’ll give them a few minutes.”
They gave them five minutes, and then crept very quietly down the passage.
“Go round by the dining-room, Bunter,” said his lordship; “they may bolt that way.”
With infinite precaution, he unlocked and opened the library door, and Gherkins noticed how silently the locks moved.
A circle of light from an electric torch was moving slowly along the bookshelves. The burglars had obviously heard nothing of the counterattack. Indeed, they seemed to have troubles enough of their own to keep their attention occupied. As his eyes grew accustomed to the dim light, Gherkins made out that one man was standing holding the torch, while the other took down and examined the books. It was fascinating to watch his apparently disembodied hands move along the shelves in the torchlight.
The man muttered discontentedly. Obviously the job was proving a harder one than they had bargained for. The habit of ancient authors of abbreviating the titles on the backs of their volumes, or leaving them completely untitled, made things extremely awkward. From time to time the man with the torch extended his hand into the light. It held a piece of paper, which they anxiously compared with the titlepage of a book. Then the volume was replaced and the tedious search went on.

===Chinese===

而彼得叔叔呢，没说“胡说，小黄瓜，快回床上去”，反而坐起来听了听，说：“天哪，小黄瓜，我看你说得对。”还派小黄瓜去叫邦特。回来时，一向把舅舅视作高礼帽式体面人物的小黄瓜，亲眼看见他从手帕抽屉里取出一把如假包换的自动手枪。
正是在这一刻，彼得勋爵从“相当不错的舅舅”升格为“神化的舅舅”。他说：
“听着，小黄瓜，不知道这帮家伙来几个，所以你务必机灵，我一声令下立刻照办——哪怕我喊‘快溜’。答应？”
小黄瓜答应了，心口咚咚直跳。两人摸黑坐着等，忽然，彼得勋爵床头正上方一只小电铃尖锐地响了，一盏绿灯亮起。
“书房窗户，”爵爷说着，一拨开关把铃掐灭。“他们若听见了，兴许知难而退。给他们几分钟。”
他们给了五分钟，随后悄没声地摸下走廊。
“绕饭厅走，邦特，”爵爷说；“他们兴许往那边跑。”
他万般小心地开锁推开门，小黄瓜注意到锁簧动得何其无声。
一环电筒光正沿书架缓缓移动。窃贼显然毫无察觉反击将至。其实，他们自家够多的事已占满了心神。眼睛适应了微光，小黄瓜辨认出：一人持电筒站着，另一人取书翻验。看那双仿佛无主的手在电筒光里沿架游走，着实迷人。
那人咕咕哝哝地不满。显然，这活比讲好的难啿。古代作者爱在书脊上缩写书名、或索性不题书名的积习，把事情搅得别扭至极。持电筒的不时把手伸进光里。手里捏着一张纸，同某本书的书名页急急比对。书放回去，乏味的搜查继续。

===Original===

Suddenly some slight noise﻿—Gherkins was sure *he* did not make it; it may have been Bunter in the dining-room﻿—seemed to catch the ear of the kneeling man.
“Wot’s that?” he gasped, and his startled face swung round into view.
“Hands up!” said Lord Peter, and switched the light on.
The second man made one leap for the dining-room door, where a smash and an oath proclaimed that he had encountered Bunter. The kneeling man shot his hands up like a marionette.
“Gherkins,” said Lord Peter, “do you think you can go across to that gentleman by the bookcase and relieve him of the article which is so inelegantly distending the right-hand pocket of his coat? Wait a minute. Don’t on any account get between him and my pistol, and mind you take the thing out *very* carefully. There’s no hurry. That’s splendid. Just point it at the floor while you bring it across, would you? Thanks. Bunter has managed for himself, I see. Now run into my bedroom, and in the bottom of my wardrobe you will find a bundle of stout cord. Oh! I beg your pardon; yes, put your hands down by all means. It must be very tiring exercise.”
The arms of the intruders being secured behind their backs with a neatness which Gherkins felt to be worthy of the best traditions of Sexton Blake, Lord Peter motioned his captives, to sit down and despatched Bunter for whiskey-and-soda.
“Before we send for the police,” said Lord Peter, “you would do me a great personal favour by telling me what you were looking for, and who sent you. Ah! thanks, Bunter. As our guests are not at liberty to use their hands, perhaps you would be kind enough to assist them to a drink. Now then, say when.”
“Well you’re a gentleman, guv’nor,” said the First Burglar, wiping his mouth politely on his shoulder, the back of his hand not being available. “If we’d a known wot a job this wos goin’ ter be, blow me if we’d a touched it. The bloke said, ses ’e, ‘It’s takin’ candy from a baby,’ ’e ses. ‘The gentleman’s a reg’lar softie,’ ’e ses, ‘one o’ these ’ere sersiety toffs wiv a maggot fer old books,’ that’s wot ’e ses, ‘an’ ef yer can find this ’ere old book fer me, ’e ses, ‘there’s a pony fer yer.’ Well! Sech a job! ’E didn’t mention as ’ow there’d be five ’undred fousand bleedin’ ole books all as alike as a regiment o’ bleedin’ dragoons.

===Chinese===

忽然，一声轻响——小黄瓜确信不是自己弄的；也许是饭厅里的邦特——钻进了跪着那人的耳朵。
“什么？”他倒抽一口气，惊惶的脸转进光里。
“举手！”彼得勋爵说，把灯打开。
另一人朝饭厅门纵身一扑——那边一声闷响加一句咒骂，宣告他撞上了邦特。跪着的那个双手直挺挺弹起，像提线木偶。
“小黄瓜，”彼得勋爵说，“你能不能过去到书柜旁那位先生身边，把他上衣右袋里撑得不好看的那个物件缴下来？慢着。千万站在他同我的手枪的连线之外，取的时候务必非常小心。不用急。好极了。拿过来时枪口朝地，行么？多谢。看得出邦特自己料理好了。现在跑去我卧室，衣柜底层有一捆粗绳。哦！对不住；是，手放下尽管放下。这姿势想必很累。”
两位不速之客的双臂被反剪捆好，手法之利落，小黄瓜觉得足配塞克斯顿·布莱克的最优传统。彼得勋爵示意俘虏坐下，派邦特去取威士忌苏打。
“在叫警察之前，”彼得勋爵说，“二位若肯告诉我你们找什么、谁差你们来的，便帮了我个人一个大忙。啊！多谢，邦特。咱们的客人双手不便，劳驾你喂他们喝一口。好了，满了说一声。”
“您可真是位君子，长官，”大贼说——手背用不上，礼貌地拿肩膀擦了擦嘴。“早知道这活这么难缠，打死我们也不接。那家伙说，他说：‘这跟从娃娃手里抢糖一个样，’他说。‘那位爷是个十足的面瓜，’他说，‘就是那种迷老书的上流款爷’，他说，‘你们要能把这本老书给我找着，’他说，‘二十五镑归你们。’好家伙！这叫什么活！他可没提这儿会有五十万本破老书，一个模子刻的，跟一团龙骑兵似的。

===Original===

Nor as ’ow yer kept a nice little machine-gun like that ’andy by the bedside, *nor* yet as ’ow yer was so bleedin’ good at tyin’ knots in a bit o’ string. No﻿—’e didn’t think ter mention them things.”
“Deuced unsporting of him,” said his lordship. “Do you happen to know the gentleman’s name?”
“No﻿—that was another o’ them things wot ’e didn’t mention. ’E’s a stout, fair party, wiv ’orn rims to ’is goggles and a bald ’ead. One o’ these ’ere philanthropists, I reckon. A friend o’ mine, wot got inter trouble onct, got work froo ’im, and the gentleman comes round and ses to ’im, ’e ses, ‘could yer find me a couple o’ lads ter do a little job?’ ’e ses, an’ my friend, finkin’ no ’arm, you see, guv’nor, but wot it might be a bit of a joke like, ’e gets ’old of my pal an’ me, an’ we meets the gentleman in a pub dahn Whitechapel way. W’ich we was ter meet ’im there again Friday night, us ’avin’ allowed that time fer ter git ’old of the book.”
“The book being, if I may hazard a guess, the *Cosmographia Universalis*?”
“Sumfink like that, guv’nor. I got its jaw-breakin’ name wrote down on a bit o’ paper, wot my pal ’ad in ’is ’and. Wot did yer do wiv that ’ere bit o’ paper, Bill?”
“Well, look here,” said Lord Peter, “I’m afraid I must send for the police, but I think it likely, if you give us your assistance to get hold of your gentleman, whose name I strongly suspect to be Wilberforce Pope, that you will get off pretty easily. Telephone the police, Bunter, and then go and put something on that eye of yours. Gherkins, we’ll give these gentlemen another drink, and then I think perhaps you’d better hop back to bed; the fun’s over. No? Well, put on a good thick coat, there’s a good fellow, because what your mother will say to me if you catch a cold I don’t like to think.”
So the police had come and taken the burglars away, and now Detective-Inspector Parker, of Scotland Yard, a great personal friend of Lord Peter’s, sat toying with a cup of coffee and listening to the story.
“But what’s the matter with the jolly old book, anyhow, to make it so popular?” he demanded.
“I don’t know,” replied Wimsey, “but after Mr. Pope’s little visit the other day I got kind of intrigued about it and had a look through it. I’ve got a hunch it may turn out rather valuable, after all. Unsuspected beauties and all that sort of thing. If only Mr.

===Chinese===

也没提您床头顺手搁着那么一把小巧的机关枪，更没提您拿根小绳打结打得这么地道。没有——这些事他没想到提。”
“太不讲牌品了，”爵爷说。“碰巧知道那位先生的大名么？”
“不知道——这也是他没提的事里的一件。是个白白胖胖的家伙，眼镜角质边，脑袋秃顶。我算计着，是那种慈善家。我一个朋友，从前惹过事，通过他找着了活儿；这位先生就找上门去，他说：‘你能给我寻两个小子干点小事么？’他说。我朋友呢，您瞧，长官，寻思无妨——顶多算个玩笑——就把我和我伙计找来，在白教堂那边的酒馆见了这位先生。我们还约好星期五夜里再在那儿碰头，算是留出取书的工夫。”
“那本书，容我猜一猜，是《宇宙通志》（*Cosmographia Universalis*）？”
“差不离吧，长官。那拗口的名字写在一张纸上，在我伙计手里。那张纸你弄哪儿去了，比尔？”
“这样，”彼得勋爵说，“警察恐怕必须叫；不过我想，你们若肯帮忙擒下你们那位先生——他的大名我强烈怀疑是威尔伯福斯·波普——你们的日子会好过得多。打电话叫警察，邦特，打完去给眼睛上点药。小黄瓜，咱们再请这两位先生喝一杯，然后你最好跳回床上去；戏收场了。不去？好吧，披件厚外套，好孩子——你要是着了凉，你妈会怎么说我，我想都不敢想。”
于是警察来把窃贼带走了。现在，苏格兰场的侦探帕克探长（Inspector Parker）——彼得勋爵的至交——坐着拨弄一杯咖啡，听完了全部故事。
“可这本宝贝老书到底怎么回事，这么招人？”他问。
“不知道，”温西答道，“不过波普先生那天来访之后，我起了点疑心，把它通翻了一遍。我有种预感，它说不定相当值钱。深藏不露的美玉，那一类。只可惜

===Original===

Pope had been a trifle more accurate in his facts, he might have got away with something to which I feel pretty sure he isn’t entitled. Anyway, when I’d seen﻿—what I saw, I wrote off to Dr. Conyers of Yelsall Manor, the late owner﻿—”
“Conyers, the cancer man?”
“Yes. He’s done some pretty important research in his time, I fancy. Getting on now, though; about seventy-eight, I fancy. I hope he’s more honest than his nephew, with one foot in the grave like that. Anyway, I wrote (with Gherkins’s permission, naturally) to say we had the book and had been specially interested by something we found there, and would he be so obliging as to tell us something of its history. I also﻿—”
“But what did you find in it?”
“I don’t think we’ll tell him yet, Gherkins, shall we? I like to keep policemen guessing. As I was saying, when you so rudely interrupted me, I also asked him whether he knew anything about his good nephew’s offer to buy it back. His answer has just arrived. He says he knows of nothing specially interesting about the book. It has been in the library untold years, and the tearing out of the maps must have been done a long time ago by some family vandal. He can’t think why his nephew should be so keen on it, as he certainly never pored over it as a boy. In fact, the old man declares the engaging Wilberforce has never even set foot in Yelsall Manor to his knowledge. So much for the fire-breathing monsters and the pleasant Sunday afternoons.”
“Naughty Wilberforce!”
“M’m. Yes. So, after last night’s little dustup, I wired the old boy we were tooling down to Yelsall to have a heart-to-heart talk with him about his picture-book and his nephew.”
“Are you taking the book down with you?” asked Parker. “I can give you a police escort for it if you like.”
“That’s not a bad idea,” said Wimsey. “We don’t know where the insinuating Mr. Pope may be hanging out, and I wouldn’t put it past him to make another attempt.”
“Better be on the safe side,” said Parker. “I can’t come myself, but I’ll send down a couple of men with you.”
“Good egg,” said Lord Peter. “Call up your myrmidons. We’ll get a car round at once. You’re coming, Gherkins, I suppose? God knows what your mother would say. Don’t ever be an uncle, Charles; it’s frightfully difficult to be fair to all parties.”

===Chinese===

波普先生的事实再准那么一点，他说不定真就把一件我敢肯定不属于他的东西弄到手了。总之，我见了——我见的东西之后，便给耶尔索尔庄园的康纳斯医生、原书主人去了信——”
“康纳斯？研究癌症的那位？”
“正是。当年想必做过些相当要紧的研究。如今老了；七十八了吧，我猜。一只脚已踏进坟墓，希望他比他侄儿诚实。总之我写了信（自然经小黄瓜首肯），说书在我们手上，里头见到一样东西令我们格外在意，烦请他惠示此书来历。我还——”
“可你们在里头发现了什么？”
“暂且不告诉他，好不好，小黄瓜？我就爱让警察猜。我方才说到——你打岔打得真无礼——我还问他，他那位好侄儿要赎书的事他知道不知道。回信刚到。他说不知此书有何特别可观。它在藏书室里不知放了多少年，地图早被家中哪位败家子撕去，必是很久以前的事。他想不通侄儿为何这样上心，因为他小时候绝没翻过它。老先生甚至断言：据他所知，那位讨喜的威尔伯福斯压根没踏进过耶尔索尔庄园。喷火怪和快活的星期天下午，就这么收场。”
“淘气的威尔伯福斯！”
“嗯。是。于是，昨夜那场小骚动之后，我给老先生拍了电报，说我们要驱车去耶尔索尔，同他就他的画书和他侄儿推心置腹谈一谈。”
“书带下去么？”帕克问。“需要的话，我给你派警力护送。”
“这主意不坏，”温西说。“那位会来事的波普先生蛰在哪儿，咱们不知道；再干一票，他也干得出来。”
“稳妥为上，”帕克说。“我本人来不了，派两个人随你去。”
“好极了，”彼得勋爵说。“把你的鹰犬叫来。车这就叫。你也去，小黄瓜，我猜？天知道你妈会说什么。查尔斯，千万别当舅舅；想面面公平，难得吓人。”

===Original===

---
Yelsall Manor was one of those large, decaying country mansions which speak eloquently of times more spacious than our own. The original late Tudor construction had been masked by the addition of a wide frontage in the Italian manner, with a kind of classical portico surmounted by a pediment and approached by a semicircular flight of steps. The grounds had originally been laid out in that formal manner in which grove nods to grove and each half duly reflects the other. A late owner, however, had burst out into the more eccentric sort of landscape gardening which is associated with the name of Capability Brown. A Chinese pagoda, somewhat resembling Sir William Chambers’s erection in Kew Gardens but smaller, rose out of a grove of laurustinus towards the eastern extremity of the house, while at the rear appeared a large artificial lake, dotted with numerous islands, on which odd little temples, grottos, teahouses, and bridges peeped out from among clumps of shrubs, once ornamental, but now sadly overgrown. A boathouse, with wide eaves like the designs on a willow-pattern plate, stood at one corner, its landing-stage fallen into decay and wreathed with melancholy weeds.
“My disreputable old ancestor, Cuthbert Conyers, settled down here when he retired from the sea in 1732,” said Dr. Conyers, smiling faintly. “His elder brother died childless, so the black sheep returned to the fold with the determination to become respectable and found a family. I fear he did not succeed altogether. There were very queer tales as where his money came from. He is said to have been a pirate, and to have sailed with the notorious Captain Blackbeard. In the village, to this day, he is remembered and spoken of as Cutthroat Conyers. It used to make the old man very angry, and there is an unpleasant story of his slicing the ears off a groom who had been heard to call him ‘Old Cutthroat.’ He was not an uncultivated person, though. It was he who did the landscape-gardening round at the back, and he built the pagoda for his telescope. He was reputed to study the Black Art, and there were certainly a number of astrological works in the library with his name on the flyleaf, but probably the telescope was only a remembrance of his seafaring days.
“Anyhow, towards the end of his life he became more and more odd and morose.

===Chinese===

---
耶尔索尔庄园是那种正在倾颓的大乡宅，雄辩地诉说着一个比我们这时代更恢廓的时代。都铎晚期的老屋被一道意大利式宽阔门面遮住了，配着古典柱廊、山花顶，由一道半圆石阶引入。园子最初按规整式布置，树影对树影，两两相照如镜。后来的一位主人却大兴“万能布朗”式的奇僻造园之风。一座中国宝塔——同邱园里威廉·钱伯斯爵士那座相仿而稍小——从一片月桂树丛中拔起，在宅子东端；宅后则是一片浩大的人工湖，点缀着无数岛屿，岛上一座座古怪的小庙、洞窟、茶室、小桥，从一丛丛昔日齐整、如今疯长得不成体统的灌木间探头探脑。一角立着船屋，飞檐宽展，宛如柳纹瓷盘上的画样；码头朽坏，缠满愁惨的水草。
“我那位不光彩的老祖先卡斯伯特·康纳斯，一七三二年从海上退隐后，便在此落脚，”康纳斯医生淡淡笑着说。“他兄长无嗣而终，这头害群之马便带着洗心革面、成家立业的决心归了圈。恐怕他没能全遂愿。他的钱从哪儿来，传闻稀奇古怪。据说他当过海盗，跟着那恶名昭著的黑胡子船长跑过船。直到今天，村里人提起他，还是叫他‘割喉康纳斯’。老头子生前为此大动肝火，还有一段不愉快的掌故：他割掉过一个马夫的耳朵，只因那家伙叫人听见喊他‘老割喉’。不过他并非没有教养。宅后那片园子便是他布置的，宝塔是为他的望远镜盖的。相传他研习黑术，藏书室里确实有好多部占星书，扉页上都有他的名字；不过那望远镜，多半只是对航海岁月的纪念。
“总之，到了晚年，他愈发古怪阴沉。

===Original===

He quarrelled with his family, and turned his younger son out of doors with his wife and children. An unpleasant old fellow.
“On his deathbed he was attended by the parson﻿—a good, earnest, God-fearing sort of man, who must have put up with a deal of insult in carrying out what he firmly believed to be the sacred duty of reconciling the old man to this shamefully treated son. Eventually, ‘Old Cutthroat’ relented so far as to make a will, leaving to the younger son ‘My treasure which I have buried in Münster.’ The parson represented to him that it was useless to bequeath a treasure unless he also bequeathed the information where to find it, but the horrid old pirate only chuckled spitefully, and said that, as he had been at the pains to collect the treasure, his son might well be at the pains of looking for it. Further than that he would not go, and so he died, and I dare say went to a very bad place.
“Since then the family has died out, and I am the sole representative of the Conyers, and heir to the treasure, whatever and wherever it is, for it was never discovered. I do not suppose it was very honestly come by, but, since it would be useless now to try and find the original owners, I imagine I have a better right to it than anybody living.
“You may think it very unseemly, Lord Peter, that an old, lonely man like myself should be greedy for a hoard of pirate’s gold. But my whole life has been devoted to studying the disease of cancer, and I believe myself to be very close to a solution of one part at least of the terrible problem. Research costs money, and my limited means are very nearly exhausted. The property is mortgaged up to the hilt, and I do most urgently desire to complete my experiments before I die, and to leave a sufficient sum to found a clinic where the work can be carried on.
“During the last year I have made very great efforts to solve the mystery of ‘Old Cutthroat’s’ treasure. I have been able to leave much of my experimental work in the most capable hands of my assistant, Dr. Forbes, while I pursued my researches with the very slender clue I had to go upon. It was the more expensive and difficult that Cuthbert had left no indication in his will whether Münster in Germany or Münster in Ireland was the hiding-place of the treasure. My journeys and my search in both places cost money and brought me no further on my quest.

===Chinese===

他同家人反目，把小儿子连同妻儿撵出了门。一个不痛快的老家伙。
“临终时，牧师守在他床边——一位善良、恳挚、敬畏上帝的人；他坚信使老人同蒙冤的儿子和解是神圣义务，为此受的气想必少不了。最后，‘老割喉’总算软了心，立下遗嘱，留给小儿子‘吾埋藏于明斯特之财宝’。牧师向他进言：遗下财宝而不遗下寻找之法，等于白遗。可那老海盗只是恶毒地唂唂一笑，说：既然搜集财宝的辛苦他受过了，寻宝的辛苦就不妨留给儿子。再多一个字也不肯说，就这么死了——我料想，去了一个很糟的地方。
“打那以后，家族凋零，我是康纳斯家仅存的苗裔，也是那笔财宝的继承人——无论它是什么、在哪里，反正从没被发现过。我不指望它来路多么清白；可如今再想寻访原主已属徒劳，我想，比之世上任何活人，我对它有更好的权利。
“彼得勋爵，您也许觉得，我这样孤老头子还觊觎一窖海盗黄金，有失体统。可我毕生致力于癌症研究，而且我相信，那道可怕难题至少有一部分，我已濒临解出。研究要钱，而我有限的资财几乎耗尽。产业抵押到了顶，我极盼死前完成实验，并留下一笔足够的钱，创办一所把这事业延续下去的诊所。
“去年一年，我为解开‘老割喉’宝藏之谜下了大力气。实验工作多托付给我的助手福布斯医生——最能干的一个人——我自己凭着仅有的一点蛛丝马迹四处追索。麻烦在于，卡斯伯特遗嘱里根本没指明藏宝处是德国的明斯特，还是爱尔兰的明斯特，这使搜寻加倍费钱费力。两地都跑遍了，钱花了，追寻却毫无长进。

===Original===

I returned, disheartened, in August, and found myself obliged to sell my library, in order to defray my expenses and obtain a little money with which to struggle on with my sadly delayed experiments.”
“Ah!” said Lord Peter. “I begin to see light.”
The old physician looked at him enquiringly. They had finished tea, and were seated around the great fireplace in the study. Lord Peter’s interested questions about the beautiful, dilapidated old house and estate had led the conversation naturally to Dr. Conyer’s family, shelving for the time the problem of the *Cosmographia*, which lay on a table beside them.
“Everything you say fits into the puzzle,” went on Wimsey, “and I think there’s not the smallest doubt what Mr. Wilberforce Pope was after, though how he knew that you had the *Cosmographia* here, I couldn’t say.”
“When I disposed of the library, I sent him a catalogue,” said Dr. Conyers. “As a relative, I thought he ought to have the right to buy anything he fancied. I can’t think why he didn’t secure the book then, instead of behaving in this most shocking fashion.”
Lord Peter hooted with laughter.
“Why, because he never tumbled to it till afterwards,” he said. “And oh, dear, how wild he must have been! I forgive him everything. Although,” he added, “I don’t want to raise your hopes too high, sir, for, even when we’ve solved old Cuthbert’s riddle, I don’t know that we’re very much nearer to the treasure.”
“To the *treasure*?”
“Well, now, sir. I want you first to look at this page, where there’s a name scrawled in the margin. Our ancestors had an untidy way of signing their possessions higgledy-piggledy in margins instead of in a decent, Christian way in the flyleaf. This is a handwriting of somewhere about Charles I’s reign: ‘Jac: Coniers.’ I take it that goes to prove that the book was in the possession of your family at any rate as early as the first half of the seventeenth century, and has remained there ever since. Right. Now we turn to page 1099, where we find a description of the discoveries of Christopher Columbus. It’s headed, you see, by a kind of map, with some of Mr. Pope’s monsters swimming about in it, and apparently representing the Canaries, or, as they used to be called, the Fortunate Isles.

===Chinese===

八月里，我心灰意冷地回来，不得已卖掉了藏书，偿付开销，再筹一点钱，让严重耽搁的实验勉强续下去。”
“啊！”彼得勋爵说。“我看见亮了。”
老医生询问地望着他。茶已用罢，三人围坐在书房大壁炉旁。彼得勋爵对这座破落老宅和产业的一连串问题，使话题自然而然引到了康纳斯的家史，《宇宙通志》之谜暂且靠边——书就摊在手边的桌上。
“您说的每一件都对上了这盘谜，”温西接着说，“威尔伯福斯·波普先生图什么，我看毫无疑义；只是他怎么知道《宇宙通志》在您这儿，我说不上。”
“处理藏书时，我给他寄了份目录，”康纳斯医生说。“念在亲戚份上，我想他该有优先购下心爱之物的权利。想不通他当时为何不把书定下，反倒闹出这般骇人的把戏。”
彼得勋爵哈哈大笑。
“嗨，因为他到事后才回过味来呗，”他说。“哦天哪，他得气成什么样！我全原谅他了。不过，”他补了一句，“我不想把您的指望抬太高，先生——即便解开老卡斯伯特的谜，我们离财宝近了多少，也还难说。”
“离财宝？”
“是这样，先生。先请您看这一页，页边上涂着一个名字。我们的祖先署名认领物件，不规规矩矩写在扉页，倒爱乱涂在页边。这笔迹大约是查理一世年间：‘Jac: Coniers’（雅各·科尼尔斯）。我想这足证：至迟十七世纪上半叶，此书已入贵府，且从此未离。好。现在翻到一〇九九页，是讲克里斯托弗·哥伦布发现的章节。您看，开头配着一幅地图，波普先生那些怪物在里头游来游去；画的显然是加那利群岛——旧称‘幸运群岛’。

===Original===

It doesn’t look much more accurate than old maps usually are, but I take it the big island on the right is meant for Lanzarote, and the two nearest to it may be Teneriffe and Gran Canaria.”
“But what’s that writing in the middle?”
“That’s just the point. The writing is later than ‘Jac: Coniers’s’ signature; I should put it about 1700﻿—but, of course, it may have been written a good deal later still. I mean, a man who was elderly in 1730 would still use the style of writing he adopted as a young man, especially if, like your ancestor the pirate, he had spent the early part of his life in outdoor pursuits and hadn’t done much writing.”
“Do you mean to say, Uncle Peter,” broke in the viscount excitedly, “that that’s ‘Old Cutthroat’s’ writing?”
“I’d be ready to lay a sporting bet it is. Look here, sir, you’ve been scouring round Münster in Germany and Münster in Ireland﻿—but how about good old Sebastian Münster here in the library at home?”
“God bless my soul! Is it possible?”
“It’s pretty nearly certain, sir. Here’s what he says, written, you see, round the head of that sort of sea-dragon:

> *Hic in capite draconis ardet perpetuo Sol.*

Here the sun shines perpetually upon the Dragon’s Head.

===Chinese===

准头同旧地图的通常水准差不多；不过我猜，右边那座大岛是兰萨罗特，挨着它的两座，大概是特内里费和大加那利。”
“可中间那行字是什么？”
“这正是要害。这行字晚于‘Jac: Coniers’的签名；我断在一七〇〇年前后——当然，实际书写还可以晚得多。我是说，一个人一七三〇年上了年纪，笔迹仍会是年轻时的路数——尤其像您那位海盗先祖，前半生干的是露天的营生，不大动笔。”
“彼得叔叔，您的意思是，”子爵激动地插进来，“那是‘老割喉’的手笔？”
“我敢下注赌它是。您瞧，先生：您跑遍了德国的明斯特、爱尔兰的明斯特——可怎么没想到家里书房这位老塞巴斯蒂安·明斯特呢？”
“我的天！竟有此可能？”
“几乎可以断定，先生。他写的话在这儿——您看，就绕着那头海龙的龙头：

> *Hic in capite draconis ardet perpetuo Sol.*

此处，太阳永照龙头。

===Original===

Rather doggy Latin﻿—sea-dog Latin, you might say, in fact.”
“I’m afraid,” said Dr. Conyers, “I must be very stupid, but I can’t see where that leads us.”
“No; ‘Old Cutthroat’ was rather clever. No doubt he thought that, if anybody read it, they’d think it was just an allusion to where it says, further down, that ‘the islands were called *Fortunatae* because of the wonderful temperature of the air and the clemency of the skies.’ But the cunning old astrologer up in his pagoda had a meaning of his own. Here’s a little book published in 1678﻿—Middleton’s *Practical Astrology*﻿—just the sort of popular handbook an amateur like ‘Old Cutthroat’ would use. Here you are: ‘If in your figure you find Jupiter or Venus or *Dragon’s head*, you may be confident there is Treasure in the place supposed﻿—If you find *Sol* to be the Significator of the hidden Treasure, you may conclude there is Gold, or some jewels.’ You know, sir, I think we may conclude it.”
“Dear me!” said Dr. Conyers. “I believe, indeed, you must be right. And I am ashamed to think that if anybody had suggested to me that it could ever be profitable to me to learn the terms of astrology, I should have replied in my vanity that my time was too valuable to waste on such foolishness. I am deeply indebted to you.”
“Yes,” said Gherkins, “but where *is* the treasure, uncle?”
“That’s just it,” said Lord Peter. “The map is very vague; there is no latitude or longitude given; and the directions, such as they are, seem not even to refer to any spot on the islands, but to some place in the middle of the sea. Besides, it is nearly two hundred years since the treasure was hidden, and it may already have been found by somebody or other.”
Dr. Conyers stood up.
“I am an old man,” he said, “but I still have some strength. If I can by any means get together the money for an expedition, I will not rest till I have made every possible effort to find the treasure and to endow my clinic.”
“Then, sir, I hope you’ll let me give a hand to the good work,” said Lord Peter.
---
Dr. Conyers had invited his guests to stay the night, and, after the excited viscount had been packed off to bed, Wimsey and the old man sat late, consulting maps and diligently reading Münster’s chapter “*De Novis Insulis*,” in the hope of discovering some further clue. At length, however, they separated, and Lord Peter went upstairs, the book under his arm.

===Chinese===

拉丁文颇带海狗腔——老水手拉丁文，可以说。”
“只怕我太愚钝，”康纳斯医生说，“看不出这能把我们引向哪里。”
“看不出，才对；‘老割喉’相当高明。他必定盘算过：谁读到它，都当只是照应下文那句——‘诸岛因空气温煦、天宇和暖，得名*Fortunatae*（幸运岛）’。可宝塔上那个狡黠的老占星家自有私意。这儿有本小书，一六七八年版——米德尔顿的《实用占星术》——正是‘老割喉’这路业余爱好者会用的那种通俗手册。您听：‘若于命盘中见木星、金星或龙首，可确信所疑之处有宝——若见太阳为藏宝之征星，可断其地为黄金，或珠宝。’您知道么，先生，我想咱们可以断一断了。”
“天哪！”康纳斯医生说。“我相信您必定是对的。说来惭愧：若有人从前劝我，说学占星术语于我有利，我定会自负地答，我的光阴太宝贵，不能抛掷在这等蠢事上。受您大恩了。”
“是呀，”小黄瓜说，“可宝藏在哪儿呢，叔叔？”
“症结正在这儿，”彼得勋爵说。“地图语焉不详；经纬度一概没有；那点指示，看来指的甚至不是岛上哪一点，而是海当间儿的某个地方。何况藏宝至今将近两百年，保不齐早叫什么人找去了。”
康纳斯医生站起身。
“我是老人了，”他说，“可还有些力气。只要能想方设法凑出远征的款项，我必不歇息，直到竭尽一切努力寻到宝藏，为我诊所置下根基。”
“那么，先生，这桩善举，望您容我搭把手。”彼得勋爵说。
---
康纳斯医生留客过夜。兴奋的子爵被打发上床后，温西同老人又坐到深夜，查对地图，勤读明斯特《论新诸岛》一章，指望再找出些提示。终于各自散去，彼得勋爵夹着书上楼。

===Original===

He was restless, however, and, instead of going to bed, sat for a long time at his window, which looked out upon the lake. The moon, a few days past the full, was riding high among small, windy clouds, and picked out the sharp eaves of the Chinese teahouses and the straggling tops of the unpruned shrubs. “Old Cutthroat” and his landscape-gardening! Wimsey could have fancied that the old pirate was sitting now beside his telescope in the preposterous pagoda, chuckling over his riddling testament and counting the craters of the moon. “If *Luna*, there is silver.” The water of the lake was silver enough; there was a great smooth path across it, broken by the sinister wedge of the boathouse, the black shadows of the islands, and, almost in the middle of the lake, a decayed fountain, a writhing Celestial dragon-shape, spiny-backed and ridiculous.
Wimsey rubbed his eyes. There was something strangely familiar about the lake; from moment to moment it assumed the queer unreality of a place which one recognises without having ever known it. It was like one’s first sight of the Leaning Tower of Pisa﻿—too like its picture to be quite believable. Surely, thought Wimsey, he knew that elongated island on the right, shaped rather like a winged monster, with its two little clumps of buildings. And the island to the left of it, like the British Isles, but warped out of shape. And the third island, between the others, and nearer. The three formed a triangle, with the Chinese fountain in the centre, the moon shining steadily upon its dragon head. “*Hic in capite draconis ardet perpetuo*”﻿—
Lord Peter sprang up with a loud exclamation, and flung open the door into the dressing-room. A small figure wrapped in an eiderdown hurriedly uncoiled itself from the window-seat.
“I’m sorry, Uncle Peter,” said Gherkins. “I was so *dreadfully* wide awake, it wasn’t any good staying in bed.”
“Come here,” said Lord Peter, “and tell me if I’m mad or dreaming. Look out of the window and compare it with the map﻿—Old Cutthroat’s ‘New Islands.’ He made ’em, Gherkins; he put ’em here. Aren’t they laid out just like the Canaries? Those three islands in a triangle, and the fourth down here in the corner? And the boathouse where the big ship is in the picture? And the dragon fountain where the dragon’s head is? Well, my son, that’s where your hidden treasure’s gone to.

===Chinese===

可他心绪不定，没上床，在临湖的窗前坐了许久。月亮过望几日，高悬在疾走的小云间，勾出中国茶亭的尖檐和从未修剪的灌木乱蓬蓬的顶。‘老割喉’同他的造园！温西几乎能想见老海盗此刻正坐在那座荒唐的宝塔里他的望远镜旁，为自己的谜语遗嘱窃笑，数着月亮的环形山。“逢月座，则有银。”湖水的银够瞧的了；一道宽阔平滑的光路横贯湖面，被船屋不祥的楔形、岛屿的黑影截断；而几乎正在湖心，立着一座朽坏的喷泉——一条盘曲的中国龙形，脊背带刺，滑稽可笑。
温西揉了揉眼。这湖有种奇异的熟悉；时时刻刻，它显出一种古怪的不真实——认得出、却从未到过的地方。就像初次看见比萨斜塔——同画上太像，反倒不敢信。温西想，右边那座狭长的岛他必定认得：形状像一头有翼的怪物，带着两小簇屋宇。它左边那座，像不列颠诸岛，只是走了形。还有第三座，夹在两岛之间，近些。三岛成三角，正中是那座中国喷泉，月光稳稳照着龙头。“*Hic in capite draconis ardet perpetuo*”——龙首之上，太阳恒照——
彼得勋爵一声高呼跳起来，猛开化妆室的门。一个裹着羽绒被的小身影从窗座上慌忙展开。
“对不起，彼得叔叔，”小黄瓜说。“我精神得不得了，躺着白躺。”
“过来，”彼得勋爵说，“告诉我，是我疯了还是做梦。朝窗外看，同那幅地图比——‘老割喉’的‘新诸岛’。岛是他造的，小黄瓜；他把它们安在这儿了。布置得不是同加那利一模一样么？三岛成三角，第四座在下头角落里？画里大船的位置，正是船屋？画里龙头的位置，正是龙形喷泉？好了，我的孩子，你的藏宝，就藏到那儿去了。

===Original===

Get your things on, Gherkins, and damn the time when all good little boys should be in bed! We’re going for a row on the lake, if there’s a tub in that boathouse that’ll float.”
“Oh, Uncle Peter! This is a *real* adventure!”
“All right,” said Wimsey. “Fifteen men on the dead man’s chest, and all that! Yo-ho-ho, and a bottle of Johnny Walker! Pirate expedition fitted out in dead of night to seek hidden treasure and explore the Fortunate Isles! Come on, crew!”
---
Lord Peter hitched the leaky dinghy to the dragon’s knobbly tail and climbed out carefully, for the base of the fountain was green and weedy.
“I’m afraid it’s your job to sit there and bail, Gherkins,” he said. “All the best captains bag the really interesting jobs for themselves. We’d better start with the head. If the old blighter said head, he probably meant it.” He passed an arm affectionately round the creature’s neck for support, while he methodically pressed and pulled the various knobs and bumps of its anatomy. “It seems beastly solid, but I’m sure there’s a spring somewhere. You won’t forget to bail, will you? I’d simply hate to turn round and find the boat gone. Pirate chief marooned on island and all that. Well, it isn’t its back hair, anyhow. We’ll try its eyes. I say, Gherkins, I’m sure I felt something move, only it’s frightfully stiff. We might have thought to bring some oil. Never mind; it’s dogged as does it. It’s coming. It’s coming. Booh! Pah!”
A fierce effort thrust the rusted knob inwards, releasing a huge spout of water into his face from the dragon’s gaping throat. The fountain, dry for many years, soared rejoicingly heavenwards, drenching the treasure-hunters, and making rainbows in the moonlight.
“I suppose this is ‘Old Cutthroat’s’ idea of humour,” grumbled Wimsey, retreating cautiously round the dragon’s neck. “And now I can’t turn it off again. Well, dash it all, let’s try the other eye.”
He pressed for a few moments in vain. Then with a grinding clang, the bronze wings of the monster clapped down to its sides, revealing a deep square hole, and the fountain ceased to play.
“Gherkins!” said Lord Peter, “we’ve done it. (But don’t neglect bailing on that account!) There’s a box here. And it’s beastly heavy. No; all right, I can manage. Gimme the boat-hook. Now I do hope the old sinner really did have a treasure. What a bore if it’s only one of his little jokes.

===Chinese===

穿衣裳，小黄瓜，管他什么乖孩子上床的钟点！咱们这就下湖划船——只要船屋里还有条能漂的划子。”
“哦，彼得叔叔！这才是真正的冒险！”
“好嘞，”温西说。“‘十五个人扒死人箱’，全套来一套！哟嗬嗬，再来一瓶约翰尼·沃克！海盗远征队深夜出航，寻宝藏、探幸运群岛！开船，水手们！”
---
彼得勋爵把漏水的小划子系上龙那疙疙瘩瘩的尾巴，小心爬上喷泉——泉座上青苔水草，滑腻得很。
“恐怕只能委屈你坐在那儿舀水了，小黄瓜，”他说。“最好的船长总是把顶有意思的活儿留给自己。咱们从龙头下手。老家伙既然说头，多半就是头。”他一条胳膊亲热地搂住那畜生的脖子借力，一边有条不紊地按、拉它周身上下各处疙瘩和凸起。“结实得可恨，可准有个机关在哪儿。别忘了舀水啊？回头一转身船没了，我可受不了。海盗头子被放逐荒岛云云。嗯，脊背上没有。试试眼睛。我说，小黄瓜，我肯定觉出有什么动了，就是僵得要命。该想到带点油来的。不管它；天下无难事，只怕有心人。动了。动了。嚯！呸！”
狠狠一使劲，锈死的旋钮压了进去，龙喉咙里喷出一大股水，正劈头浇在他脸上。干涸多年的喷泉欢天喜地直冲云霄，把寻宝人浇成落汤鸡，月光里挂出道道虹。
“这想必就是‘老割喉’式的幽默，”温西绕着龙脖子小心后撤，哝哝道。“这下还关不上了。见鬼，试试另一只眼。”
他按了几下，徒劳。随后一声轧轧巨响，怪物的铜翅向两翼扑落，露出一个方方的深洞，喷泉应声而止。
“小黄瓜！”彼得勋爵说，“成了。（可别因此误了舀水！）这儿有只箱子。沉得要命。不；行了，我来。拿船钩来。现在，只盼老罪人真有宝。若又是一场小玩笑，那才扫兴。

===Original===

Never mind﻿—hold the boat steady. There. Always remember, Gherkins, that you can make quite an effective crane with a boat-hook and a stout pair of braces. Got it? That’s right. Now for home and beauty.﻿ ﻿… Hullo! what’s all that?”
As he paddled the boat round, it was evident that something was happening down by the boathouse. Lights were moving about, and a sound of voices came across the lake.
“They think we’re burglars, Gherkins. Always misunderstood. Give way, my hearties﻿—

> “A-roving, a-roving, since roving’s been my ru-i-in,

I’ll go no more a-roving with you, fair maid.”

“Is that you, my lord?” said a man’s voice as they drew in to the boathouse.
“Why, it’s our faithful sleuths!” cried his lordship. “What’s the excitement?”
“We found this fellow sneaking round the boathouse,” said the man from Scotland Yard. “He says he’s the old gentleman’s nephew. Do you know him, my lord?”
“I rather fancy I do,” said Wimsey. “Mr. Pope, I think. Good evening. Were you looking for anything? Not a treasure, by any chance? Because we’ve just found one. Oh! don’t say that. *Maxima reverentia*, you know. Lord St. George is of tender years. And, by the way, thank you so much for sending your delightful friends to call on me last night. Oh, yes, Thompson, I’ll charge him all right. You there, doctor? Splendid. Now, if anybody’s got a spanner or anything handy, we’ll have a look at Great-grandpapa Cuthbert. And if he turns out to be old iron, Mr. Pope, you’ll have had an uncommonly good joke for your money.”
An iron bar was produced from the boathouse and thrust under the hasp of the chest. It creaked and burst. Dr. Conyers knelt down tremulously and threw open the lid.
There was a little pause.
“The drinks are on you, Mr. Pope,” said Lord Peter. “I think, doctor, it ought to be a jolly good hospital when it’s finished.”

===Chinese===

别管——把船稳住。起——记住喽，小黄瓜：一根船钩加一副结实的背带，就是一架像样的吊车。钩住了？好。这就打道回府……哟！那边怎么回事？”
他把船划转过来，船屋那边分明出了事。灯火晃动，人声隔着湖面传来。
“他们把咱们当贼了，小黄瓜。人总是被误解。划呀，弟兄们——

> “四海漂泊，漂泊，漂泊毁了我此生，

今生不再漂泊，随你同行，姑娘。”

“是您么，爵爷？”船靠上船屋时，一个男人问。
“哎呀，是咱们忠实的神探们！”爵爷喊。“出什么热闹了？”
“我们撞见这家伙在船屋周围鬼鬼祟祟，”苏格兰场的人说。“他自称老先生的侄儿。您认得他么，爵爷？”
“我想我认得，”温西说。“波普先生，没走眼吧。晚上好。您在找什么？不会碰巧是宝藏吧？因为我们刚找着一处。哦！别骂人。*Maxima reverentia*（大敬大畏），知道么。圣乔治勋爵年纪还嫩。对了，多谢您昨夜派那几位可爱的朋友来拜访我。哦，是的，汤普森，这项指控我一定提。您在么，医生？好极了。来人，谁手边有扳手什么的，咱们这就开箱看看曾祖父卡斯伯特。若是开出一箱子破铁，波普先生，您这笔钱买的笑话可就高级了。”
船屋里寻出一根铁棍，插进箱扣底下。吱嘎一声，绷裂了。康纳斯医生颤巍巍跪下，掠开箱盖。
小小的静默。
“酒钱记您账上，波普先生，”彼得勋爵说。“我说医生，等它落成，该是一所顶呱呱的好医院。”

## The Piscatorial Farce of the Stolen Stomach / 失窃胃囊的垂钓闹剧
===Original===

“What in the world,” said Lord Peter Wimsey, “is that?”
Thomas Macpherson disengaged the tall jar from its final swathings of paper and straw and set it tenderly upright beside the coffeepot.
“That,” he said, “is Great-Uncle Joseph’s legacy.”
“And who is Great-Uncle Joseph?”
“He was my mother’s uncle. Name of Ferguson. Eccentric old boy. I was rather a favourite of his.”
“It looks like it. Was that all he left you?”
“Imph’m. He said a good digestion was the most precious thing a man could have.”
“Well, he was right there. Is this his? Was it a good one?”
“Good enough. He lived to be ninety-five, and never had a day’s illness.”
Wimsey looked at the jar with increased respect.
“What did he die of?”
“Chucked himself out of a sixth-story window. He had a stroke, and the doctors told him﻿—or he guessed for himself﻿—that it was the beginning of the end. He left a letter. Said he had never been ill in his life and wasn’t going to begin now. They brought it in temporary insanity, of course, but I think he was thoroughly sensible.”
“I should say so. What was he when he was functioning?”
“He used to be in business﻿—something to do with shipbuilding, I believe, but he retired long ago. He was what the papers call a recluse. Lived all by himself in a little top flat in Glasgow, and saw nobody. Used to go off by himself for days at a time, nobody knew where or why. I used to look him up about once a year and take him a bottle of whisky.”
“Had he any money?”
“Nobody knew. He ought to have had﻿—he was a rich man when he retired. But, when we came to look into it, it turned out he only had a balance of about five hundred pounds in the Glasgow Bank. Apparently he drew out almost everything he had about twenty years ago. There were one or two big bank failures round about that time, and they thought he must have got the wind up. But what he did with it, goodness only knows.”
“Kept it in an old stocking, I expect.”
“I should think Cousin Robert devoutly hopes so.”
“Cousin Robert?”
“He’s the residuary legatee. Distant connection of mine, and the only remaining Ferguson. He was awfully wild when he found he’d only got five hundred. He’s rather a bright lad, is Robert, and a few thousands would have come in handy.”
“I see. Well, how about a bit of brekker? You might stick Great Uncle Joseph out of the way somewhere. I don’t care about the looks of him.”

===Chinese===

“这到底是什么？”彼得·温西勋爵（Lord Peter Wimsey）问。
托马斯·麦克弗森把那只高罐子从最后一层纸和稻草里解脱出来，轻轻地立在咖啡壶旁边。
“这个，”他说，“是约瑟夫舅公的遗产。”
“约瑟夫舅公又是哪位？”
“我母亲的叔叔。姓弗格森。古怪的老家伙。我算他挺待见的一个。”
“看得出来。他就给你留了这个？”
“可不。他说，一副好肠胃，是人能有的最金贵的东西。”
“这个嘛，他说得对。这是他的肠胃？是好肠胃？”
“不赖。他活到九十五，一天病没生过。”
温西看那罐子的眼光添了几分敬意。
“那他怎么死的？”
“从六楼窗户里跳了出去。他中风了，医生告诉他——或者他自己猜到——这是末路的开始。他留下一封信。说他这辈子从没病过，也不打算现在开这个头。当然，陪审团裁了个临时精神失常；我倒觉得他清醒得很。”
“我也这么看。他生前是干什么的？”
“从前做生意——好像是造船那一行，不过早就退了。用报上的话说，是个隐士。一个人住在格拉斯哥一套顶层小公寓里，谁也不见。常常独自出门，一走好几天，没人知道去哪儿、为什么。我过去每年去探他一回，带一瓶威士忌。”
“他有钱吗？”
“没人知道。照理该有——他退休时是阔人。可等我们一查，他格拉斯哥银行里只剩五百镑上下。看样子，约二十年前他把钱几乎全提走了。那阵子前后有过一两回银行倒闭的大风潮，他们猜他准是吓着了。钱弄哪儿去了，天晓得。”
“我猜是塞进了旧线袜。”
“罗伯特表哥怕正烧香盼着这样。”
“罗伯特表哥？”
“他是剩余遗产继承人。我的一个远亲，弗格森家最后一根苗。他发现自己只捞着五百镑，气得够啺。罗伯特是个挺灵的小伙子，几千镑对他不无小补。”
“明白了。那么，来点早饭？你把约瑟夫舅公攴一边去。他那副尊容我看不惯。”

===Original===

“I thought you were rather partial to anatomical specimens.”
“So I am, but not on the breakfast-table. ‘A place for everything and everything in its place,’ as my grandmother used to say. Besides, it would give Maggie a shock if she saw it.”
Macpherson laughed, and transferred the jar to a cupboard.
“Maggie’s shock-proof. I brought a few odd bones and things with me, by way of a holiday task. I’m getting near my final, you know. She’ll just think this is another of them. Ring the bell, old man, would you? We’ll see what the trout’s like.”
The door opened to admit the housekeeper, with a dish of grilled trout and a plate of fried scones.
“These look good, Maggie,” said Wimsey, drawing his chair up and sniffing appreciatively.
“Aye, sir, they’re gude, but they’re awfu’ wee fish.”
“Don’t grumble at them,” said Macpherson. “They’re the sole result of a day’s purgatory up on Loch Whyneon. What with the sun fit to roast you and an east wind, I’m pretty well flayed alive. I very nearly didn’t shave at all this morning.” He passed a reminiscent hand over his red and excoriated face. “Ugh! It’s a stiff pull up that hill, and the boat was going wallop, wallop all the time, like being in the Bay of Biscay.”
“Damnable, I should think. But there’s a change coming. The glass is going back. We’ll be having some rain before we’re many days older.”
“Time, too,” said Macpherson. “The burns are nearly dry, and there’s not much water in the Fleet.” He glanced out of the window to where the little river ran tinkling and skinkling over the stones at the bottom of the garden. “If only we get a few days’ rain now, there’ll be some grand fishing.”
“It *would* come just as I’ve got to go, naturally,” remarked Wimsey.
“Yes; can’t you stay a bit longer? I want to have a try for some sea-trout.”
“Sorry, old man, can’t be done. I must be in town on Wednesday. Never mind. I’ve had a fine time in the fresh air and got in some good rounds of golf.”
“You must come up another time. I’m here for a month﻿—getting my strength up for the exams and all that. If you can’t get away before I go, we’ll put it off till August and have a shot at the grouse. The cottage is always at your service, you know, Wimsey.”
“Many thanks. I may get my business over quicker than I think, and, if I do, I’ll turn up here again. When did you say your great-uncle died?”
Macpherson stared at him.

===Chinese===

“我还当你挺偏爱解剖标本。”
“偏爱归偏爱，可不在早餐桌上。‘物各有位，各归其位’，我祖母常说。再说，叫玛吉瞧见了要吓一跳。”
麦克弗森笑着把罐子挪进碗柜。
“玛吉惊不倒。我随身带了几根零碎骨头什么的，当假期功课。我的毕业考快到了，晓得吧。她只当这又是其中一件。按铃，老兄，劳驾？看看鳟鱼怎么样。”
门开了，管家端进一盘烤鳟鱼、一碟烤饼。
“这看着就好，玛吉。”温西拖椅子上桌，欣赏地嗅着。
“是嘞先生，鱼好，就是小得可怜。”
“别嫌了，”麦克弗森说。“这是我在怀尼恩湖上炼了一整天的狱，独一份的收成。太阳烤得人冒油，又顶着东风，我快给剥了层皮。今天早上差一点索性不刮脸了。”他怀念地摸了摸那张红红的、脱了皮的脸。“呸！爬那道坡是真累人，船一路哐当哐当颠，跟在比斯开湾里似的。”
“要我说，够受的。不过天要变了。晴雨表在回落。不出几天就要下雨。”
“也该下了，”麦克弗森说。“溪沟都快干了，弗利特河里水也不多。”他朝窗外瞥了一眼——小河在花园尽头，在石头上叮叮咚咚地淌。“这会儿若能下他几天雨，钓鱼才叫美。”
“偏赶上我要走，它自然要来。”温西说。
“是啊；不能多留几天？我还想试试海鳟呢。”
“抱歉，老兄，不成。星期三我必须到城里。不要紧。新鲜空气我享受够了，高尔夫也打了个痛快。”
“下回一定再来。我在这儿待一个月——养足精神应付考试，等等。我走前你脱不开身的话，推到八月，打松鸡。这农舍随时候着你，温西。”
“多谢。我的事也许办得比预想快；真是那样，我就再摸过来。你说舅公是几时去世的来着？”
麦克弗森瞪着他。

===Original===

“Some time in April, as far as I can remember. Why?”
“Oh, nothing﻿—I just wondered. You were a favourite of his, didn’t you say?”
“In a sense. I think the old boy liked my remembering him from time to time. Old people are pleased by little attentions, you know.”
“M’m. Well, it’s a queer world. What did you say his name was?”
“Ferguson﻿—Joseph Alexander Ferguson, to be exact. You seem extraordinarily interested in Great-Uncle Joseph.”
“I thought, while I was about it, I might look up a man I know in the shipbuilding line, and see if he knows anything about where the money went to.”
“If you can do that, Cousin Robert will give you a medal. But, if you really want to exercise your detective powers on the problem, you’d better have a hunt through the flat in Glasgow.”
“Yes﻿—what is the address, by the way?”
Macpherson told him the address.
“I’ll make a note of it, and, if anything occurs to me, I’ll communicate with Cousin Robert. Where does he hang out?”
“Oh, he’s in London, in a solicitor’s office. Crosbie & Plump, somewhere in Bloomsbury. Robert was studying for the Scottish Bar, you know, but he made rather a mess of things, so they pushed him off among the Sassenachs. His father died a couple of years ago﻿—he was a Writer to the Signet in Edinburgh﻿—and I fancy Robert has rather gone to the bow-wows since then. Got among a cheerful crowd down there, don’t you know, and wasted his substance somewhat.”
“Terrible! Scotsmen shouldn’t be allowed to leave home. What are you going to do with Great-Uncle?”
“Oh, I don’t know. Keep him for a bit, I think. I liked the old fellow, and I don’t want to throw him away. He’ll look rather well in my consulting-room, don’t you think, when I’m qualified and set up my brass plate. I’ll say he was presented by a grateful patient on whom I performed a marvellous operation.”
“That’s a good idea. Stomach-grafting. Miracle of surgery never before attempted. He’ll bring sufferers to your door in flocks.”
“Good old Great-Uncle﻿—he may be worth a fortune to me after all.”
“So he may. I don’t suppose you’ve got such a thing as a photograph of him, have you?”
“A photograph?” Macpherson stared again. “Great-Uncle seems to be becoming a passion with you. I don’t suppose the old man had a photograph taken these thirty years. There was one done then﻿—when he retired from business. I expect Robert’s got that.”

===Chinese===

“记得是四月里某天。怎么？”
“哦，没什么——随便问问。你说过，你是他待见的人？”
“某种意义上。我想老头子喜欢我时时记着他。老人嘛，小殷勤就受用，晓得吧。”
“唔。这世道也怪。你说他叫什么来着？”
“弗格森——约瑟夫·亚历山大·弗格森，全称。你对约瑟夫舅公的兴趣可非同小可。”
“既然赶上了，我想顺道去打听个造船行的熟人，看他知不知道那笔钱的下落。”
“你若打听出来，罗伯特表哥该给你发枚奖章。不过你当真想在那难题上过过侦探瘾，最好去格拉斯哥那套公寓翻一翻。”
“是——对了，地址是哪儿？”
麦克弗森告诉了他。
“我记下。有了什么主意，就同罗伯特表哥联络。他住在哪儿？”
“哦，他在伦敦，一家律师事务所。克罗斯比-普伦普，在布卢姆斯伯里什么地方。罗伯特本来在读苏格兰律师，搞砸了，家里就把他推给撒克逊佬了。他父亲——爱丁堡的御玺文书——前两年去世；我看打那以后，罗伯特就有点往下出溜。在南边交了一帮快活朋友，晓得吧，家底折腾掉了一些。”
“可怕！苏格兰人不该放他们离家。舅公你打算怎么处置？”
“哦，没想好。先留一阵吧。我喜欢那老头，不想把他扔了。等我考出来、挂上铜牌，他摆在我的诊室里倒挺体面，你说是不是？我就说，他是一位感激的病人敬赠的——我在他身上做过一桩神乎其神的大手术。”
“好主意。胃移植。外科史上未有的奇迹。病人要成群结队堵你的门。”
“好舅公——到头来他也许给我带来一笔家当。”
“谁说不是。你手头不会有他一张照片吧？”
“照片？”麦克弗森又瞪眼。“舅公简直要变成你的心头好了。老头子这三十年怕是没照过相。当年倒是照过一张——他退休那会儿。想必在罗伯特手里。”

===Original===

“Och aye,” said Wimsey, in the language of the country.
---
Wimsey left Scotland that evening, and drove down through the night towards London, thinking hard as he went. He handled the wheel mechanically, swerving now and again to avoid the green eyes of rabbits as they bolted from the roadside to squat fascinated in the glare of his headlamps. He was accustomed to say that his brain worked better when his immediate attention was occupied by the incidents of the road.
Monday morning found him in town with his business finished and his thinking done. A consultation with his shipbuilding friend had put him in possession of some facts about Great-Uncle Joseph’s money, together with a copy of Great-Uncle Joseph’s photograph, supplied by the London representative of the Glasgow firm to which he had belonged. It appeared that old Ferguson had been a man of mark in his day. The portrait showed a fine, dour old face, long-lipped and high in the cheekbones﻿—one of those faces which alter little in a lifetime. Wimsey looked at the photograph with satisfaction as he slipped it into his pocket and made a beeline for Somerset House.
Here he wandered timidly about the wills department, till a uniformed official took pity on him and enquired what he wanted.
“Oh, thank you,” said Wimsey effusively, “thank you so much. Always feel nervous in these places. All these big desks and things, don’t you know, so awe-inspiring and businesslike. Yes, I just wanted to have a squint at a will. I’m told you can see anybody’s will for a shilling. Is that really so?”
“Yes, sir, certainly. Anybody’s will in particular, sir?”
“Oh, yes, of course﻿—how silly of me. Yes. Curious, isn’t it, that when you’re dead any stranger can come and snoop round your private affairs﻿—see how much you cut up for and who your lady friends were, and all that. Yes. Not at all nice. Horrid lack of privacy, what?”
The attendant laughed.
“I expect it’s all one when you’re dead, sir.”
“That’s awfully true. Yes, naturally, you’re dead by then and it doesn’t matter. May be a bit trying for your relations, of course, to learn what a bad boy you’ve been. Great fun annoyin’ one’s relations. Always do it myself. Now, what were we sayin’? Ah! yes﻿—the will. (I’m always so absentminded.) Whose will, you said?

===Chinese===

“可不是嘛。”温西用当地的土话应道。
---
当晚温西离开苏格兰，连夜驱车南下伦敦，一路苦苦地想。他机械地打着方向盘，不时一让，避开兔子那双绿灯似的眼睛——它们从路边窜出，又在车灯里蹲住不动。他常说，当眼前被路上的种种事故占满时，他的脑子转得最灵。
星期一早晨，他人在城里，事已办完，想也想透了。同造船行那位朋友一商量，他掌握了约瑟夫舅公那笔钱的几样事实，还拿到一张约瑟夫舅公的照片，是他原先那家格拉斯哥商行的伦敦代表提供的。看来老弗格森当年是个人物。相片上一张倔强的老脸，气度堂堂，唇长颧高——一生少改的那一类脸。温西满意地看着照片，揣进兜里，直奔萨默塞特宫。
在遗嘱部门，他怯生生地转来转去，直到一位制服官员动了恻隐，问他要什么。
“哦，多谢，”温西热络地说，“太感谢了。这些地方我总发怵。这么多大桌子什么的，晓得吧，威风凛凛，公事公办。是，我只想瞄一眼一份遗嘱。听说一先令（shilling）就能看随便谁的遗嘱。真有这事？”
“有的，先生，当然。特别要看哪一位的，先生？”
“哦，是，当然——瞧我多糊涂。是。说来也怪，人一死，随便哪个生人都能来扒你的私事——看你刮下多少油水，你的相好都是谁，等等。是。不怎么体面。隐私上头，缺得很，呃？”
侍者笑了。
“我想要是死了，也就都无所谓了，先生。”
“这话对极了。是啊，人到那时已经死了，自然无所谓。当然，对亲戚们可能有点扎心，叫他们知道你原来这么不成器。气亲戚，其乐无穷。我就常干。咦，咱们说到哪儿了？啊！对——遗嘱。（我这人总是这么心不在焉。）你说，谁的遗嘱？”

===Original===

Well, it’s an old Scots gentleman called Joseph Alexander Ferguson that died at Glasgow﻿—you know Glasgow, where the accent’s so strong that even Scotsmen faint when they hear it﻿—in April, this last April as ever was. If it’s not troubling you too much, may I have a bob’s-worth of Joseph Alexander Ferguson?”
The attendant assured him that he might, adding the caution that he must memorise the contents of the will and not on any account take notes. Thus warned, Wimsey was conducted into a retired corner, where in a short time the will was placed before him.
It was a commendably brief document, written in holograph, and was dated the previous January. After the usual preamble and the bequest of a few small sums and articles of personal ornament to friends, it proceeded somewhat as follows:

> And I direct that, after my death, the alimentary organs be removed entire with their contents from my body, commencing with the oesophagus and ending with the anal canal, and that they be properly secured at both ends with a suitable ligature, and be enclosed in a proper preservative medium in a glass vessel and given to my great-nephew Thomas Macpherson of the Stone Cottage, Gatehouse-of-the-Fleet, in Kirkcudbrightshire, now studying medicine in Aberdeen. And I bequeath him these my alimentary organs with their contents for his study and edification, they having served me for ninety-five years without failure or defect, because I wish him to understand that no riches in the world are comparable to the riches of a good digestion. And I desire of him that he will, in the exercise of his medical profession, use his best endeavours to preserve to his patients the blessing of good digestion unimpaired, not needlessly filling their stomachs with drugs out of concern for his own pocket, but exhorting them to sober and temperate life agreeably to the design of Almighty Providence.

===Chinese===

是位苏格兰老绅士，叫约瑟夫·亚历山大·弗格森，殁于格拉斯哥——晓得格拉斯哥吧，口音重得连苏格兰人听了都要晕过去——四月里，就是刚过去的这个四月。若不太麻烦，给我来一先令的约瑟夫·亚历山大·弗格森。”
侍者担保可以，又告诫他务必记在脑子里，万万不可抄录。得了这一警告，温西被领进一处僻静角落，不多时，遗嘱摆到了他面前。
这是份简短得可嘉的文件，亲笔全文，日期是前一年一月。套话之后，是给友人几笔小款和几件随身饰物，接下来大意如此：

> 我嘱托，在我死后，将消化道连同内容物自我尸体完整取出，自食管始，至肛管止；两端以合适的结扎妥善扎紧，盛入适当的保存液，置于玻璃器皿中，赠予我的外甥孙托马斯·麦克弗森，柯库布里郡弗利特河畔门豪斯石屋，现在阿伯丁习医。我将这些连同内容物的消化道遗赠给他，供其研习与策励；此套器官为我效力九十五年，无一日怠工，无一处缺损；因我愿他明白：世上任何财富，比不得一副好肠胃的财富。我并期望他，在行医之时，竭尽全力保全病人无亏的好消化这一福分，切勿出于一己私囊之虑，无谓地拿药去填病人的胃，而当劬勉他们过清醒节制的生活，以合全能天意之设计。

===Original===

After this remarkable passage, the document went on to make Robert Ferguson residuary legatee without particular specification of any property, and to appoint a firm of lawyers in Glasgow executors of the will.
Wimsey considered the bequest for some time. From the phraseology he concluded that old Mr. Ferguson had drawn up his own will without legal aid, and he was glad of it, for its wording thus afforded a valuable clue to the testator’s mood and intention. He mentally noted three points: the “alimentary organs with their contents” were mentioned twice over, with a certain emphasis; they were to be ligatured top and bottom; and the legacy was accompanied by the expression of a wish that the legatee should not allow his financial necessities to interfere with the conscientious exercise of his professional duties. Wimsey chuckled. He felt he rather liked Great-Uncle Joseph.
He got up, collected his hat, gloves, and stick, and advanced with the will in his hand to return it to the attendant. The latter was engaged in conversation with a young man, who seemed to be expostulating about something.
“I’m sorry sir,” said the attendant, “but I don’t suppose the other gentleman will be very long. Ah!” He turned and saw Wimsey. “Here is the gentleman.”
The young man, whose reddish hair, long nose, and slightly sodden eyes gave him the appearance of a dissipated fox, greeted Wimsey with a disagreeable stare.
“What’s up? Want me?” asked his lordship airily.
“Yes, sir. Very curious thing, sir; here’s a gentleman enquiring for that very same document as you’ve been studying, sir. I’ve been in this department fifteen years, and I don’t know as I ever remember such a thing happening before.”
“No,” said Wimsey, “I don’t suppose there’s much of a run on any of your lines as a rule.”
“It’s a very curious thing indeed,” said the stranger, with marked displeasure in his voice.
“Member of the family?” suggested Wimsey.
“I *am* a member of the family,” said the foxy-faced man. “May I ask whether *you* have any connection with us?”
“By all means,” replied Wimsey graciously.
“I don’t believe it. I don’t know you.”
“No, no﻿—I meant you might ask, by all means.”
The young man positively showed his teeth.
“Do you mind telling me who you are, anyhow, and why you’re so damned inquisitive about my great-uncle’s will?”
Wimsey extracted a card from his case and presented it with a smile.

===Chinese===

这段惊世骇俗的文字之后，遗嘱指定罗伯特·弗格森为剩余遗产继承人，未列明任何具体财产，并指定格拉斯哥一家律师行充任遗嘱执行人。
温西把这项遗赠琢磨了好一阵。从措辞看，他断定老弗格森没请律师，遗嘱是自己拟的；他为此高兴，因为这样一来，字句便是立遗嘱人心绪与用意的宝贵提示（clue）。他在心里记下三点：“消化道连同内容物”提了两遍，带几分强调；要上下两端结扎；遗赠之外还附带一个愿望——受赠人不可让经济上的窘迫妨害职业良知的行使。温西暗笑。他觉得他相当喜欢约瑟夫舅公。
他起身，收好帽子、手套和手杖，拿着遗嘱走向侍者交还。侍者正同一个年轻人谈话，那人像在为什么事抗议。
“对不起，先生，”侍者说，“不过另一位先生想必很快就好。啊！”他转身看见温西。“这位先生来了。”
那年轻人红头发、长鼻子、眼睛微微泡着，活像一只喝高了的狐狸，不痛快地瞪了温西一眼。
“什么事？找我？”这位勋爵大大咧咧地问。
“是，先生。巧得很，先生；这位先生打听的，正是您方才研读的那份文件。小的在这部门待了十五年，不记得出过这样的巧事。”
“是啊，”温西说，“你们各条货品想必平时都不怎么抢手。”
“实在是巧得很。”陌生人说，话音里透着明显的不悦。
“府上的亲友？”温西试探。
“我正是府上的亲友。”狐狸脸说。“可否请问，您同我们家有什么相干？”
“尽管问。”温西和颜悦色。
“我不信。我不认得你。”
“不，不——我是说，你尽管问。”
那年轻人干脆品出了牙。
“你到底是谁？为什么对我舅公的遗嘱这么穷追不舍，能不能赏个话？”
温西从名片匣里抽出一张，含笑奉上。

Mr. Robert Ferguson changed colour.
“If you would like a reference as to my respectability,” went on Wimsey affably, “Mr. Thomas Macpherson will, I am sure, be happy to tell you about me. I am inquisitive,” said his lordship﻿—“a student of humanity. Your cousin mentioned to me the curious clause relating to your esteemed great-uncle’s﻿—er﻿—stomach and appurtenances. Curious clauses are a passion with me. I came to look it up and add it to my collection of curious wills. I am engaged in writing a book on the subject﻿—*Clauses and Consequences*. My publishers tell me it should enjoy a ready sale. I regret that my random jottings should have encroached upon your doubtless far more serious studies. I wish you a very good morning.”
As he beamed his way out, Wimsey, who had quick ears, heard the attendant informing the indignant Mr. Ferguson that he was “a very funny gentleman﻿—not quite all there, sir.” It seemed that his criminological fame had not penetrated to the quiet recesses of Somerset House. “But,” said Wimsey to himself, “I am sadly afraid that Cousin Robert has been given food for thought.”
---
Under the spur of this alarming idea, Wimsey wasted no time, but took a taxi down to Hatton Garden, to call upon a friend of his. This gentleman, rather curly in the nose and fleshy about the eyelids, nevertheless came under Mr. Chesterton’s definition of a nice Jew, for his name was neither Montagu nor McDonald, but Nathan Abrahams, and he greeted Lord Peter with a hospitality amounting to enthusiasm.
“So pleased to see you. Sit down and have a drink. You have come at last to select the diamonds for the future Lady Peter, eh?”
“Not yet,” said Wimsey.
“No? That’s too bad. You should make haste and settle down. It is time you became a family man. Years ago we arranged I should have the privilege of decking the bride for the happy day. That is a promise, you know. I think of it when the fine stones pass through my hands. I say, ‘That would be the very thing for my friend Lord Peter.’ But I hear nothing, and I sell them to stupid Americans who think only of the price and not of the beauty.”
“Time enough to think of the diamonds when I’ve found the lady.”
Mr. Abrahams threw up his hands.
“Oh, yes! And then everything will be done in a hurry! ‘Quick, Mr. Abrahams! I have fallen in love yesterday and I am being married tomorrow.’

罗伯特·弗格森先生变了脸色。
“若需担保我人还端正，”温西接着和蕥地说，“托马斯·麦克弗森先生一定乐于奉告。我这人好奇，”这位勋爵说——“是个研究人性的。令亲同我提起贵舅公那一条——呃——胃及附件的奇特条款。奇特条款是我的嗜好。我来查一查，收进我那本奇特遗嘱集子。我正在写这么一本书——《条款及其后果》。出版商说销路一定好。可惜我的乱涂乱记，搅扰了您想必郑重得多的研究。祝您早安。”
他笑吟吟往外走，耳朵尖的温西听见侍者正向怒气冲冲的弗格森先生介绍：他是“一位顶好笑的先生——脑子不大齐全，先生”。看来他刑侦学的名头，还没传进萨默塞特宫这些清净角落。“不过，”温西自语，“我怕是给罗伯特表哥送去了一份精神食粮。”
---
被这个惊人的念头鞭策着，温西不敢耽搁，雇车直奔哈顿花园，去访他的一位朋友。此人鼻子略弯、眼皮肉厚，却当得起切斯特顿先生“好犹太人”的定义，因为他既不叫蒙塔古也不叫麦克唐纳，而叫内森·亚伯拉罕斯；他迎接彼得勋爵，殷勤得近乎热烈。
“真高兴见你。坐，喝一杯。你到底来给未来的彼得夫人挑钻石了，嗯？”
“还没呢。”温西说。
“没有？太不像话。你该抓紧成家。早该当家立业了。多年前我们讲好的，大喜的日子，由我来给新娘子添妆。这可是答应了的事。好石头过手的时候我就想：‘这正配我朋友彼得勋爵。’可左等右等没动静，只好卖给那些只认价钱不认美的美国冤大头。”
“等找着了那位夫人，再想钻石不迟。”
亚伯拉罕斯先生双手一摊。
“哦，是了！到那时什么都得赶！‘快呀，亚伯拉罕斯先生！我昨天恋的爱，明天就结婚。’

===Original===

But it may take months﻿—years﻿—to find and match perfect stones. It can’t be done between today and tomorrow. Your bride will be married in something ready-made from the jeweller’s.”
“If three days are enough to choose a wife,” said Wimsey, laughing, “one day should surely be enough for a necklace.”
“That is the way with Christians,” replied the diamond-merchant resignedly. “You are so casual. You do not think of the future. Three days to choose a wife! No wonder the divorce-courts are busy. My son Moses is being married next week. It has been arranged in the family these ten years. Rachel Goldstein, it is. A good girl, and her father is in a very good position. We are all very pleased, I can tell you. Moses is a good son, a very good son, and I am taking him into partnership.”
“I congratulate you,” said Wimsey heartily. “I hope they will be very happy.”
“Thank you, Lord Peter. They will be happy, I am sure. Rachel is a sweet girl and very fond of children. And she is pretty, too. Prettiness is not everything, but it is an advantage for a young man in these days. It is easier for him to behave well to a pretty wife.”
“True,” said Wimsey. “I will bear it in mind when my time comes. To the health of the happy pair, and may you soon be an ancestor. Talking of ancestors, I’ve got an old bird here that you may be able to tell me something about.”
“Ah, yes! Always delighted to help you in any way, Lord Peter.”
“This photograph was taken some thirty years ago, but you may possibly recognise it.”
Mr. Abrahams put on a pair of horn-rimmed spectacles, and examined the portrait of Great-Uncle Joseph with serious attention.
“Oh, yes, I know him quite well. What do you want to know about him, eh?” He shot a swift and cautious glance at Wimsey.
“Nothing to his disadvantage. He’s dead, anyhow. I thought it just possible he had been buying precious stones lately.”
“It is not exactly business to give information about a customer,” said Mr. Abrahams.
“I’ll tell you what I want it for,” said Wimsey. He lightly sketched the career of Great-Uncle Joseph, and went on: “You see, I looked at it this way. When a man gets a distrust of banks, what does he do with his money? He puts it into property of some kind. It may be land, it may be houses﻿—but that means rent, and more money to put into banks. He is more likely to keep it in gold or notes, or to put it into precious stones.

===Chinese===

“可挑石头配石头，要几个月——几年！哪能今天明天之间办妥？你的新娘只好戴珠宝行现成的东西上礼堂。”
“三天挑得中老婆，”温西笑道，“一天总挑得中一条项链。”
“基督徒就是这个德行，”钻石商听天由命地说。“你们太随便。不为将来打算。三天挑老婆！难怪离婚法院生意兴隆。我儿子摩西下礼拜结婚。家里张罗了十年了。娶的是拉结·戈德斯坦。好姑娘，她父亲也体面。我可以告诉你，我们全家都欢喜。摩西是好儿子，顶好的儿子，我要收他入伙。”
“恭喜恭喜，”温西由衷地说，“祝他们白头偕老。”
“谢谢你，彼得勋爵。他们一定幸福，我拿得准。拉结是个甜姑娘，又爱孩子。人也好俊。俊不是一切，可对如今的年轻人是一利。对着俊太太，人也容易学好。”
“是这么回事，”温西说，“轮到我，我记住这条。为这幸福的一对干一杯，也祝您早日当上老祖宗。说起老祖宗，我带了只老鸟儿来，您兴许能给我说说道道。”
“啊，好说！凡有吩咐，无不乐意，彼得勋爵。”
“这张照片摄于约三十年前，不过您没准认得出。”
亚伯拉罕斯先生戴上一副角框眼镜，郑重其事地端详约瑟夫舅公的相片。
“哦，认得，熟得很。您想打听他什么，嗯？”他朝温西飞快而审慎地一瞥。
“不是他的短处。人反正死了。我只是想，他近来兴许买过宝石。”
“把主顾的事说出去，不算正经买卖。”亚伯拉罕斯先生说。
“我告诉您我要它做什么。”温西说着，把约瑟夫舅公的生平轻轻勾了一笔，又道：“您看，我是这么想的：一个人对银行起了疑心，钱怎么处置？换成某种财产。兴许置地，兴许置房——可那要收租，收了租又是钱，还得进银行。他更可能存金子、钞票，或者买宝石。”

===Original===

Gold and notes are comparatively bulky; stones are small. Circumstances in this case led me to think he might have chosen stones. Unless we can discover what he did with the money, there will be a great loss to his heirs.”
“I see. Well, if it is as you say, there is no harm in telling you. I know you to be an honourable man, and I will break my rule for you. This gentleman, Mr. Wallace﻿—”
“Wallace, did he call himself?”
“That was not his name? They are funny, these secretive old gentlemen. But that is nothing unusual. Often, when they buy stones, they are afraid of being robbed, so they give another name. Yes, yes. Well, this Mr. Wallace used to come to see me from time to time, and I had instructions to find diamonds for him. He was looking for twelve big stones, all matching perfectly and of superb quality. It took a long time to find them, you know.”
“Of course.”
“Yes. I supplied him with seven altogether, over a period of twenty years or so. And other dealers supplied him also. He is well known in this street. I found the last one for him﻿—let me see﻿—in last December, I think. A beautiful stone﻿—beautiful! He paid seven thousand pounds for it.”
“Some stone. If they were all as good as that, the collection must be worth something.”
“Worth anything. It is difficult to tell how much. As you know, the twelve stones, all matched together, would be worth far more than the sum of the twelve separate prices paid for the individual diamonds.”
“Naturally they would. Do you mind telling me how he was accustomed to pay for them?”
“In Bank of England notes﻿—always﻿—cash on the nail. He insisted on discount for cash,” added Mr. Abrahams, with a chuckle.
“He was a Scotsman,” replied Wimsey. “Well, that’s clear enough. He had a safe-deposit somewhere, no doubt. And, having collected the stones, he made his will. That’s clear as daylight, too.”
“But what has become of the stones?” enquired Mr. Abrahams, with professional anxiety.
“I think I know that too,” said Wimsey. “I’m enormously obliged to you, and so, I fancy, will his heir be.”
“If they should come into the market again﻿—” suggested Mr. Abrahams.
“I’ll see you have the handling of them,” said Wimsey promptly.
“That is kind of you,” said Mr. Abrahams. “Business is business. Always delighted to oblige you. Beautiful stones﻿—beautiful.

金子钞票嫌大，宝石小。这桩案子的种种情形叫我想到，他多半挑了宝石。查不清他把钱弄哪儿去了，他的继承人可要大吃亏。”
“明白了。唔，若真如您所说，告诉您也无妨。您是体面人，我为您破一回例。这位先生——华莱士先生——”
“华莱士？他报的这个名？”
“不是他的本名？这些守口的老先生，真是妙。不过也不稀奇。他们买石头，常怕遭抢，就报个假名。是，是。喨，这位华莱士先生从前时不时来找我，托我替他物色钻石。他要找十二颗大钻，颗颗配得严丝合缝，成色一等。您知道，找了很久。”
“自然。”
“是啊。前后二十年上下，我一共供了他七颗。别的行家也供过他。这条街上他都出名。最后一颗是我替他找的——我想想——该是去年十二月。一颗美钻——美！他付了七千镑。”
“好家伙。颗颗都这样，这一套可值了大钱。”
“值多少不好说。您知道，十二颗配成一套，远贵过十二颗单卖之和。”
“理当如此。他惯用什么方式付款？”
“英格兰银行钞票——回回如此——现钱当场两讫。他还非要现金折扣。”亚伯拉罕斯先生添一句，咯咯一笑。
“苏格兰人嘛。”温西答道。“好，这一层清清楚楚。不用说，他在哪儿租了保险箱。石头收齐了，他立下遗嘱。这也明白如昼。”
“可石头现在哪儿呢？”亚伯拉罕斯先生问，带着行家的焦灼。
“这个我想我也知道。”温西说。“欠您天大的人情；我想他的继承人也会欠您的。”
“它们若再回到市场——”亚伯拉罕斯先生提点。
“经手的一定是您。”温西斩钉截铁。
“您真客气。”亚伯拉罕斯先生说。“生意归生意。为您效劳，无任欢迎。美钻——美。

===Original===

If you thought of being the purchaser, I would charge you a special commission, as my friend.”
“Thank you,” said Wimsey, “but as yet I have no occasion for diamonds, you know.”
“Pity, pity,” said Mr. Abrahams. “Well, very glad to have been of service to you. You are not interested in rubies? No? Because I have something very pretty here.”
He thrust his hand casually into a pocket, and brought out a little pool of crimson fire like a miniature sunset.
“Look nice in a ring, now, wouldn’t it?” said Mr. Abrahams. “An engagement ring, eh?”
Wimsey laughed, and made his escape.
He was strongly tempted to return to Scotland and attend personally to the matter of Great-Uncle Joseph, but the thought of an important book sale next day deterred him. There was a manuscript of Catullus which he was passionately anxious to secure, and he never entrusted his interests to dealers. He contented himself with sending a wire to Thomas Macpherson:

> Advise opening up Greatuncle Joseph immediately.

===Chinese===

您若想到自己要做买主，看朋友份上，我给您一个特别佣金。”
“谢谢，”温西说，“不过眼下我还没有用钻石的场合，晓得吧。”
“可惜，可惜。”亚伯拉罕斯先生说。“好，很高兴帮上了忙。红宝石您不感兴趣？不？我这儿有件顶俏的东西。”
他随手往兜里一伸，掏出一小汪绯红的火，像一缩千倍的落日。
“嵌个戒指，多好，是不是？”亚伯拉罕斯先生说。“订婚戒指，嗯？”
温西大笑，夺门而出。
他极想立刻回苏格兰，亲自料理约瑟夫舅公那桩事，但想到第二天一场要紧的书籍拍卖，忍住了。有一部卡图卢斯的手稿，他志在必得；而他从不把自己的利益托付给书商。他只给托马斯·麦克弗森拍了封电报：

> 劝君即刻开膛舅公约瑟夫。

===Original===

The girl at the post-office repeated the message aloud and rather doubtfully. “Quite right,” said Wimsey, and dismissed the affair from his mind.
---
He had great fun at the sale next day. He found a ring of dealers in possession, happily engaged in conducting a knockout. Having lain low for an hour in a retired position behind a large piece of statuary, he emerged, just as the hammer was falling upon the Catullus for a price representing the tenth part of its value, with an overbid so large, prompt, and sonorous that the ring gasped with a sense of outrage. Skrymes﻿—a dealer who had sworn an eternal enmity to Wimsey, on account of a previous little encounter over a Justinian﻿—pulled himself together and offered a fifty-pound advance. Wimsey promptly doubled his bid. Skrymes overbid him fifty again. Wimsey instantly jumped another hundred, in the tone of a man prepared to go on till Doomsday. Skrymes scowled and was silent. Somebody raised it fifty more; Wimsey made it guineas and the hammer fell. Encouraged by this success, Wimsey, feeling that his hand was in, romped happily into the bidding for the next lot, a *Hypnerotomachia* which he already possessed, and for which he felt no desire whatever. Skrymes, annoyed by his defeat, set his teeth, determining that, if Wimsey was in the bidding mood, he should pay through the nose for his rashness. Wimsey, entering into the spirit of the thing, skied the bidding with enthusiasm. The dealers, knowing his reputation as a collector, and fancying that there must be some special excellence about the book that they had failed to observe, joined in wholeheartedly, and the fun became fast and furious. Eventually they all dropped out again leaving Skrymes and Wimsey in together. At which point Wimsey, observing a note of hesitation in the dealer’s voice, neatly extricated himself and left Mr. Skyrmes with the baby. After this disaster, the ring became sulky and demoralised and refused to bid at all, and a timid little outsider, suddenly flinging himself into the arena, became the owner of a fine fourteenth-century missal at bargain price. Crimson with excitement and surprise, he paid for his purchase and ran out of the room like a rabbit, hugging the missal as though he expected to have it snatched from him.

===Chinese===

邮局里的姑娘把电文朗声念了一遍，将信将疑。“没错。”温西说，随即把这事丢在了脑后。
---
第二天拍卖会上他玩得痛快。他发现一伙书商结成了围标的圈子，正得意洋洋地压价。他在一尊大雕像背后觅了个僻静角落猫了一个钟头，待卡图卢斯落槌在十分之一价上时翩然出手，加价之高、之快、之声若洪钟，惊得围标圈子倒抽冷气，齐呼岂有此理。斯凯姆斯——一个书商，先前为一部查士丁尼同温西小小交过一回手，从此誓不两立——定了定神，加五十镑。温西旋即加倍。斯凯姆斯再加五十。温西立刻跳涨一百，那口气像准备竞到世界末日。斯凯姆斯沉脸闭嘴。有人又添五十；温西改成基尼计价，槌落。旗开得胜，温西自觉手风正顺，兴冲冲又扑向下一件拍品——一部他早已藏有的《寻爱绮梦》，其实毫无想要的意思。斯凯姆斯输得恼火，咬定牙关：温西既然来了兴头，就叫他为自己的莽撞大放血。温西也来了劲，兴高采烈地把价抬上天。众书商久闻这位收藏大家的名头，只道那本书必有他们没看出的特别好处，也倾力加入，战况白热。最后众人再度纷纷退出，只剩斯凯姆斯同温西单挑。就在此刻，温西听出书商嗓音里一丝迟疑，干净利落地抽身，把这块烫手的婴儿留给了斯凯姆斯先生。经此一役，围标圈子蕪了，干脆拒绝出价；一个怯生生的小外行猛地杀进场子，捡了个大便宜，成了一部精美的十四世纪弥撒书的主人。他激动得满脸紫涨，付了钱，兔子似的蹿出屋子，把弥撒书紧紧掳着，活怕谁一把抢了去。

===Original===

Wimsey thereupon set himself seriously to acquire a few fine early printed books, and, having accomplished this, retired, covered with laurels and hatred.
After this delightful and satisfying day, he felt vaguely hurt at receiving no ecstatic telegram from Macpherson. He refused to imagine that his deductions had been wrong, and supposed rather that the rapture of Macpherson was too great to be confined to telegraphic expression and would come next day by post. However, at eleven next morning the telegram arrived. It said:

> Just got your wire what does it mean greatuncle stolen last night burglar escaped please write fully.

Wimsey committed himself to a brief comment in language usually confined to the soldiery. Robert had undoubtedly got Great-Uncle Joseph, and, even if they could trace the burglary to him, the legacy was by this time gone forever. He had never felt so furiously helpless. He even cursed the Catullus, which had kept him from going north and dealing with the matter personally.
While he was meditating what to do, a second telegram was brought in. It ran:

> Greatuncle’s bottle found broken in fleet dropped by burglar in flight contents gone what next.

Wimsey pondered this.
“Of course,” he said. “If the thief simply emptied the bottle and put Great-Uncle in his pocket, we’re done. Or if he’s simply emptied Great-Uncle and put the contents in his pocket, we’re done. But ‘dropped in flight’ sounds rather as though Great-Uncle had gone overboard lock, stock, and barrel. Why can’t the fool of a Scotsman put a few more details into his wires? It’d only cost him a penny or two. I suppose I’d better go up myself. Meanwhile a little healthy occupation won’t hurt him.”
He took a telegraph form from the desk and despatched a further message:

> Was greatuncle in bottle when dropped if so drag river if not pursue burglar probably Robert Ferguson spare no pains starting for Scotland tonight hope arrive early tomorrow urgent important put your back into it will explain.

===Chinese===

温西接着正儿八经地收进了几部精美的早期印本，功成身退，载誉而归，也结了一身仇。
痛快圆满的一天过后，竟没等来麦克弗森狂喜的电报，他隐约觉得受了伤。他不肯想自己的推理错了，只当麦克弗森的狂喜大到电报盛不下，要等第二天信到。然而第二天上午十一点，电报到了。写着：

> 电悉舅公何意昨夜被盗贼窃去逃逸乞详告。

温西用了一句通常只属于行伍的话来发表评论。罗伯特准是把约瑟夫舅公弄到手了；就算把盗窃案追到他头上，那份遗产此刻也已永远没了。他从来没有这么狂怒又这么无能为力过。他连卡图卢斯都咒了——就是它害他没能北上亲自了断。
他正琢磨怎么办，第二封电报送到了。写着：

> 舅公瓶已在弗利特河寻获摔碎系贼奔逃时失落内容物无存下一步如何。

温西沉吟。
“这还用说，”他说。“贼若只是倒空瓶子、把舅公揣进兜里，咱们完了。或者他只是掏空了舅公、把内容物揣进兜里，咱们也完了。可‘奔逃时失落’听着倒像舅公连人带装整个下了水。这苏格兰傻子怎么不肯在电报里多写几个字？多花不了几个便士。看来只好我亲自跑一趟。先给他派点有益的活计，不碍事。”
他从桌上拿起一张电报纸，又发一封：

> 舅公失落时在瓶内乎在则捞河不在则追贼疑为罗伯特·弗格森不遗余力今晚北上明晨可到紧要万望卖力容当面详。

The night express decanted Lord Peter Wimsey at Dumfries early the following morning, and a hired car deposited him at the Stone Cottage in time for breakfast. The door was opened to him by Maggie, who greeted him with hearty cordiality:
“Come awa’ in, sir. All’s ready for ye, and Mr. Macpherson will be back in a few minutes, I’m thinkin’. Ye’ll be tired with your long journey, and hungry, maybe? Aye. Will ye tak’ a bit parritch to your eggs and bacon? There’s nae troot the day, though yesterday was a gran’ day for the fush. Mr. Macpherson has been up and doun, up and doun the river wi’ my Jock, lookin’ for ane of his specimens, as he ca’s them, that was dropped by the thief that cam’ in. I dinna ken what the thing may be﻿—my Jock says it’s like a calf’s pluck to look at, by what Mr. Macpherson tells him.”
“Dear me!” said Wimsey. “And how did the burglary happen, Maggie?”
“Indeed, sir, it was a vera’ remarkable circumstance. Mr. Macpherson was awa’ all day Monday and Tuesday, up at the big loch by the viaduct, fishin’. There was a big rain Saturday and Sunday, ye may remember, and Mr. Macpherson says, ‘There’ll be grand fishin’ the morn, Jock,’ says he. ‘We’ll go up to the viaduct if it stops rainin’ and we’ll spend the nicht at the keeper’s lodge.’ So on Monday it stoppit rainin’ and was a grand warm, soft day, so aff they went together. There was a telegram come for him Tuesday mornin’, and I set it up on the mantelpiece, where he’d see it when he cam’ in, but it’s been in my mind since that maybe that telegram had something to do wi’ the burglary.”
“I wouldn’t say but you might be right, Maggie,” replied Wimsey gravely.
“Aye, sir, that wadna surprise me.” Maggie set down a generous dish of eggs and bacon before the guest and took up her tale again.
“Well, I was sittin’ in my kitchen the Tuesday night, waitin’ for Mr. Macpherson and Jock to come hame, and sair I pitied them, the puir souls, for the rain was peltin’ down again, and the nicht wes sae dark I was afraid they micht ha’ tummelt into a bog-pool. Weel, I was listenin’ for the sound o’ the door-sneck when I heard something movin’ in the front room. The door wasna lockit, ye ken, because Mr. Macpherson was expectit back. So I up from my chair and I thocht they had mebbe came in and I not heard them. I waited a meenute to set the kettle on the fire, and then I heard a crackin’ sound.

夜班快车第二天一早把彼得·温西勋爵卸在邓弗里斯，一辆雇来的车把他放在石屋农舍门口，正赶上早饭。开门的是玛吉，热热络络地迎他：
“先生快请进。都给您备好了，麦克弗森先生俺看一忽儿就回。您一路乏了，兴许还饿？是吧。鸡蛋咸肉之外，来点麦片粥不？今儿可没有鳟鱼喽，昨儿倒是钓鱼的好天。麦克弗森先生带着俺家乔克，在河里上上下下、上上下下，捞他说的什么‘标本’——就是那贼进门掉下的东西。俺是个啥也不懂——乔克说，听麦克弗森先生讲，那玩意儿瞅着像小牛的下水。”
“哎哟！”温西说。“那贼案是怎么出的，玛吉？”
“说起来，先生，可真是邪门。礼拜一礼拜二，麦克弗森先生一整天不在家，上高架桥那边大湖钓鱼去了。礼拜六礼拜天落了大雨，您兴许记得；麦克弗森先生就说：‘明儿鱼情准好，乔克，’他说，‘雨一停咱上高架桥去，夜宿守林人小屋。’于是礼拜一天一放晴，又暖和又软和，爷俩就去了。礼拜二早上来了封他的电报，俺给立在壁炉台上，他一进门就瞧得见；可俺后来心里直犯咕唧：那电报，兴许同这偷儿有点干系。”
“玛吉，你这话说不好说，可也没准儿不对。”温西正色道。
“是嘞先生，俺也不惊怪。”玛吉给客人端上满满一盘鸡蛋咸肉，接着讲。
“喨，礼拜二夜里，俺正坐俺那厨房里，等麦克弗森先生同乔克家来，怪心疼他们爷俩的，雨又泼下来了，天黑得怕人，俺直怕他们栽进泥塘里。正听着门闩响动，忽听得前屋里有动静。门没锁，您晓得，反正等着麦克弗森先生回。俺就从椅子上起来，寻思莫不是他们家来了俺没听见。俺呆了一分钟，正要把水壶坐上火，就听见咔啦一声响。

===Original===

So I cam’ out and I called, ‘Is’t you, Mr. Macpherson?’ And there was nae answer, only anither big crackin’ noise, so I ran forrit, and a man cam’ quickly oot o’ the front room, brushin’ past me an’ puttin’ me aside wi’ his hand, so, and oot o’ the front door like a flash o’ lightin’. So, wi’ that, I let oot a skelloch, an’ Jock’s voice answered me fra’ the gairden gate. ‘Och!’ I says, ‘Jock! here’s a burrglar been i’ the hoose!’ An’ I heered him runnin’ across the gairden, doun tae the river, tramplin’ doun a’ the young kail and the stra’berry beds, the blackguard!”
Wimsey expressed his sympathy.
“Aye, that was a bad business. An’ the next thing, there was Mr. Macpherson and Jock helter-skelter after him. If Davie Murray’s cattle had brokken in, they couldna ha’ done mair deevastation. An’ then there was a big splashin’ an’ crashin, an’ after a bit, back comes Mr. Macpherson an’ he says, ‘He’s jumpit intil the Fleet,’ he says, ‘an’ he’s awa’. What has he taken?’ he says. ‘I dinna ken,’ says I, ‘for it all happened sae quickly I couldna see onything.’ ‘Come awa ben,’ says he, ‘an’ we’ll see what’s missin’.’ So we lookit high and low, an’ all we could find was the cupboard door in the front room broken open, and naething taken but this bottle wi’ the specimen.”
“Aha!” said Wimsey.
“Ah! an’ they baith went oot togither wi’ lichts, but naething could they see of the thief. Sae Mr. Macpherson comes bak, and ‘I’m gaun to ma bed,’ says he, ‘for I’m that tired I can dae nae mair the nicht,’ says he. ‘Oh!’ I said ‘I daurna gae tae bed; I’m frichtened.’ An’ Jock said, ‘Hoots, wumman, dinna fash yersel’. There’ll be nae mair burglars the nicht, wi’ the fricht we’ve gied ’em.’ So we lockit up a’ the doors an’ windies an’ gaed to oor beds, but I couldna sleep a wink.”
“Very natural,” said Wimsey.
“It wasna till the next mornin’,” said Maggie, “that Mr. Macpherson opened yon telegram. Eh! but he was in a taking. An’ then the telegrams startit. Back an’ forrit, back an’ forrit atween the hoose an’ the post-office. An’ then they fund the bits o’ the bottle that the specimen was in, stuck between twa stanes i’ the river. And aff goes Mr. Macpherson an’ Jock wi’ their waders on an’ a couple o’ gaffs, huntin’ in a’ the pools an’ under the stanes to find the specimen. An’ they’re still at it.”
At this point three heavy thumps sounded on the ceiling.
“Gude save us!”

“俺就出来喊：‘是麦克弗森先生么？’没人应，又是老大一声咔啦。俺往前一跑，一条汉子从前屋里冲出来，把俺往边上一搡——这么一搡——出了前门，一道闪电似的没了影。俺放声一嗓子，乔克的声音从园子门口应了俺。‘哎呀！’俺说，‘乔克！家里进贼啦！’俺听见他跑过园子、直奔河去，把小菜秧子、草莓畦踩了个稀烂，这挨刀的！”
温西深表同情。
“是嘞，坏透了。紧跟着，麦克弗森先生同乔克撒开腿就追。戴维·默里家的牛要是闯了进来，糟蹋也不过如此。跟着就是河里扑通扑通、哗啦哗啦，过了一阵，麦克弗森先生回来了，说：‘他跳进弗利特河了，’他说，‘跑了。拿了什么？’他说。‘俺不晓得，’俺说，‘事情太快，俺啥也没看清。’‘进来，’他说，‘咱们点点缺什么。’于是俺们高高低低一通找，统共只寻见前屋碗柜门被攽开，除了盛标本的那只瓶，一样没少。”
“啊哈！”温西说。
“啊！爷俩又打着灯一齐出去，贼影子也没见。麦克弗森先生就回来了。‘俺要睡了，’他说，‘今晚累得一步也挪不动了。’‘哎呀！’俺说，‘俺不敢睡；俺怕。’乔克说：‘嗬，婆娘，别自家折腾。吓成这样，今夜哪还有贼再来。’于是俺们锁死一应门窗，各自上床，可俺一夜没合眼。”
“人之常情。”温西说。
“第二天早上，”玛吉说，“麦克弗森先生才拆那封电报。哎哟！他那个坐立不安哟。跟着电报就一趟趟地来。家里、邮局，来回跑。再跟着，他们在河里两块石头当中，寻着了装标本那瓶子的碎片。麦克弗森先生同乔克蹬上涉水裤、拎起两杆搭钩就去了，一个潭一个潭摸，一块石头一块石头翻，找那标本。这会儿还在河里呢。”
正说着，天花板上咚咚咚三记闷响。
“老天爷！”

ejaculated Maggie, “I was forgettin’ the puir gentleman.”
“What gentleman?” enquired Wimsey.
“Him that was feshed oot o’ the Fleet,” replied Maggie. “Excuse me juist a moment, sir.”
She fled swiftly upstairs. Wimsey poured himself out a third cup of coffee and lit a pipe.
Presently a thought occurred to him. He finished the coffee﻿—not being a man to deprive himself of his pleasures﻿—and walked quietly upstairs in Maggie’s wake. Facing him stood a bedroom door, half open﻿—the room which he had occupied during his stay at the cottage. He pushed it open. In the bed lay a redheaded gentleman, whose long, foxy countenance was in no way beautified by a white bandage, tilted rakishly across the left temple. A breakfast-tray stood on a table by the bed. Wimsey stepped forward with extended hand.
“Good morning, Mr. Ferguson,” said he. “This is an unexpected pleasure.”
“Good morning,” said Mr. Ferguson snappishly.
“I had no idea, when we last met,” pursued Wimsey, advancing to the bed and sitting down upon it, “that you were thinking of visiting my friend Macpherson.”
“Get off my leg,” growled the invalid. “I’ve broken my kneecap.”
“What a nuisance! Frightfully painful, isn’t it? And they say it takes years to get right﻿—if it ever does get right. Is it what they call a Potts fracture? I don’t know who Potts was, but it sounds impressive. How did you get it? Fishing?”
“Yes. A slip in that damned river.”
“Beastly. Sort of thing that might happen to anybody. A keen fisher, Mr. Ferguson?”
“So-so.”
“So am I, when I get the opportunity. What kind of fly do you fancy for this part of the country. I rather like a Greenaway’s Gadget myself. Ever tried it?”
“No,” said Mr. Ferguson briefly.
“Some people find a Pink Sisket better, so they tell me. Do you use one? Have you got your fly-book here?”
“Yes﻿—no,” said Mr. Ferguson. “I dropped it.”
“Pity. But do give me your opinion of the Pink Sisket.”
“Not so bad,” said Mr. Ferguson. “I’ve sometimes caught trout with it.”
“You surprise me,” said Wimsey, not unnaturally, since he had invented the Pink Sisket on the spur of the moment, and had hardly expected his improvisation to pass muster. “Well, I suppose this unlucky accident has put a stop to your sport for the season. Damned bad luck. Otherwise, you might have helped us to have a go at the Patriarch.”
“What’s that? A trout?”
“Yes﻿—a frightfully wily old fish.

她一溜烟上了楼。温西给自己斟了第三杯咖啡，点起烟斗。
不多时他想起一件事。他把咖啡喝完——他可不是肯亏待自己口福的人——蹑着脚跟在玛吉身后上了楼。迎面一间卧室门半开——正是他上回小住的那间。他推开门。床上躺着一位红头发先生，那张长长的狐狸脸，被一条歪戴在左额上的白绷带弄得更无看头。床边小桌上一份早餐托盘。温西趋前伸出手。

“早上好。”弗格森先生没好气。

“是。在那条鬼河里滑了一跤。”

“没有。”弗格森先生言简意赅。

Lurks about in the Fleet. You never know where to find him. Any moment he may turn up in some pool or other. I’m going out with Mac to try for him today. He’s a jewel of a fellow. He grinned amiably, and turned to answer a shout from the stairs.
“Hullo! Wimsey! is that you?”
“It is. How’s sport?”
Macpherson came up the stairs four steps at a time, and met Wimsey on the landing as he emerged from the bedroom.
“I say, d’you know who that is? It’s Robert.”
“I know. I saw him in town. Never mind him. Have you found Great-Uncle?”
“No, we haven’t. What’s all this mystery about? And what’s Robert doing here? What did you mean by saying he was the burglar? And why is Great-Uncle Joseph so important?”
“One thing at a time. Let’s find the old boy first. What have you been doing?”
“Well, when I got your extraordinary messages I thought, of course, you were off your rocker.” (Wimsey groaned with impatience.) “But then I considered what a funny thing it was that somebody should have thought Great-Uncle worth stealing, and thought there might be some sense in what you said, after all.” (“Dashed good of you,” said Wimsey.) “So I went out and poked about a bit, you know. Not that I think there’s the faintest chance of finding anything, with the river coming down like this. Well, I hadn’t got very far﻿—by the way, I took Jock with me. I’m sure he thinks I’m mad, too. Not that he says anything; these people here never commit themselves﻿—”
“Confound Jock! Get on with it.”
“Oh﻿—well, before we’d got very far, we saw a fellow wading about in the river with a rod and a creel. I didn’t pay much attention, because, you see, I was wondering what you﻿—Yes. Well! Jock noticed him and said to me, ‘Yon’s a queer kind of a fisherman, I’m thinkin’.’ So I had a look, and there he was, staggering about among the stones with his fly floating away down the stream in front of him; and he was peering into all the pools he came to, and poking about with a gaff. So I hailed him, and he turned round, and then he put the gaff away in a bit of a hurry and started to reel in his line. He made an awful mess of it,” added Macpherson appreciatively.
“I can believe it,” said Wimsey.

麦克弗森一步四级蹿上楼，在楼梯口迎着从卧室出来的温西。

“知道。城里见过他。别管他。舅公找着没有？”

“一件一件来。先把老头子找着。你们都干什么了？”

“哦——好。我们没走多远，就瞧见一条汉子在河里蹚来蹚去，拎着钓竿和鱼篓。我起先没大留意，因为，你晓得，我正琢磨你——是。嘿！乔克瞅见他了，同我说：‘那是个啥路数钓鱼的，俺瞅着。’我一看，可不是么：他在石头里深一脚浅一脚地踉跄，毛钩顺水漂在前头；逢潭就凑上去瞅，还拿搭钩捅来捅去。我就喊他；他一回头，慌里慌张把搭钩收起来，开始绕线。绕得那叫一个惨不忍睹。”麦克弗森品评道。
